Bordism of flow modules and exact Lagrangians
Noah Porcelli, Ivan Smith

Abstract
Abstract: For a stably framed Liouville manifold X X , we construct a ‘Donaldson-Fukaya category over the sphere spectrum’ \scrF ​ ( X , 𝕊 ) \scrF(X;\mathbb{S}) . The objects are closed exact Lagrangians whose stable Gauss maps are nullhomotopic compatibly with the ambient stable framing, and the morphisms are bordism classes of framed flow modules over Lagrangian Floer flow categories; this is enriched in modules over the framed bordism ring. We develop an obstruction theory for lifting quasi-isomorphisms in the usual Fukaya category to quasi-isomorphisms in appropriate truncations or quotients of \scrF ​ ( X , 𝕊 ) \scrF(X;\mathbb{S}) . Applications include constraints on the smooth structure of exact Lagrangians in certain cotangent bundles and plumbings, constructing of non-trivial symplectic mapping classes which act trivially on the integral Fukaya category for a wide class of affine varieties, and ruling out some autoequivalences of the Fukaya categories of certain plumbings from being induced by Liouville automorphisms.



Contents

1
Introduction

1.1
Context

This paper belongs to the developing subject of ‘Floer homotopy theory’ in the sense of
[
CJS95
,
AB
]
.
Constructing homotopical refinements of Floer theory has been a longstanding goal in the subject.
The recent advances of Abouzaid-Blumberg
[
AB
]
, Bai-Xu
[
BX22
]
, Rezchikov
[
Rez22
]
and others have all focussed on the Hamiltonian case: building on work of Large
[
Lar
]
, here we use Floer homotopy theory in the Lagrangian setting, away from the case of cotangent bundles where methods of generating functions are available. It would be interesting to develop an alternative approach to these questions using microlocal sheaves
[
NS22
,
Jin20
]
.

Our purpose is two-fold:

1.

We give a self-contained construction of an associative and unital ‘spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category’
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
for a Liouville manifold
X
with
TX
stably trivial, whose objects are Lagrangians
L
whose stable Gauss map is trivial compatibly with the ambient stable framing (‘spectral Lagrangian branes’). The morphism groups of
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
are graded modules over the framed bordism ring
\Omega_{*}^{fr}(\ast)
of a point, and quasi-isomorphism in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
retains information about the framed bordism class of
L
in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)
.

2.

We develop an obstruction theory for lifting quasi-isomorphisms in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
of the graded Lagrangians underlying spectral Lagrangian branes, to quasi-isomorphisms in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
, or more generally in certain ‘Baas-Sullivan truncations’ of
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
. This has a number of concrete applications to symplectic topology, detailed in Section
1.2
.

Our work has at least two very substantial intellectual debts.

1.

On the philosophical side, we follow the mantra of Abouzaid and Blumberg to do ‘Floer homotopy without homotopy’ and define the morphism groups of
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
via bordism classes of ‘flow modules’, close cousins of the flow categories defined by compactified moduli spaces of holomorphic strips.

2.

On the technical side, we appeal to work of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta, Ono
[
FOOOar
]
, cf. also
[
LS19
]
, and Large
1
1

1

Large’s thesis also constructs a spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category, not using the language of flow modules but staying closer to spectra. We have been heavily influenced by his work, but have found it easier to pin down some details in our chosen setting.

[
Lar
]
asserting that compactified moduli spaces of Floer strips (and some more general holomorphic curves) in an exact manifold are smooth manifolds with corners; moreover, for spectral branes, those manifolds with corners are themselves stably framed compatibly with breaking. We summarise these results in Section
8
.

The first point above means that the interpretation of our ‘spectral Fukaya category’ in terms of spectra is strictly conjectural (but is expected to follow from ongoing work of Abouzaid and Blumberg).

Theorem
2
illustrates how one can apply this technology to concrete problems. It seems reasonable to expect further development in these directions.

{rmk}

Because of our intended applications, we focus on constructing a Donaldson-Fukaya category “over the sphere spectrum” assuming a stable framing on
X
. For any Liouville symplectic manifold with
2c_{1}(X)=0
, our technology also yields a category
\scrF(X;MO)
whose objects are arbitrary compact exact Lagrangians, and which is enriched in
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector spaces which are modules over the unoriented bordism ring
\Omega_{*}(\ast)
.

Abouzaid and Blumberg are developing a quasi-category (stable
\infty
-category) of Flow categories and flow bimodules. A key point of this paper is that it is already interesting to construct and investigate the spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category (i.e. without the
A_{\infty}
-structure). Crucially, it is possible to perform at least some computations, by leveraging information from the ‘ordinary’ Fukaya category. That leveraging relies on the fact that spectra, or flow modules, admit truncation functors, in which one retains the information from moduli spaces of flow lines only up to a certain dimension
k
. When
k=1
the theory is equivalent to the classical theory of Floer cochain complexes, and there is an obstruction theory which governs when one can pass information ‘up the chain’ between successive truncations. Under suitable degree hypotheses, the obstructions vanish for elementary reasons.

In our applications, most obstructions vanish for degree reasons, but not all: one must be killed ‘geometrically’. For this, we develop an analogue in flow modules of working over a quotient of the sphere spectrum, namely we consider bordism classes of ‘flow modules with Baas-Sullivan singularities’, inspired by the classical Baas-Sullivan theory
[
Baa73
,
Per15
]
of bordism for manifolds allowed to have singularities
\mathrm{Cone}(P)
modelled on cones over some specified collection of manifolds
\{P\}
.

Concretely, our applications focus on Lagrangian homology spheres, where the combination of degree arguments and working with Baas-Sullivan singularities together suffice to kill all obstructions.

1.2
Results

Let
\Omega_{n}^{fr}
denote the
n
-th framed cobordism group, which is also the
n
-th stable stem
\pi_{n}^{st}=\pi_{n}(\mathbb{S})
. More generally we write
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(Y)
for the framed bordism group of a topological space
Y
.

Let
X
be a Liouville manifold with
TX
stably trivial as a complex vector bundle. A ‘spectral Lagrangian brane’ (Definition
129
) is a closed exact Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset X
equipped with a nullhomotopy of its stable Lagrangian Gauss map which is compatible with the given stable framing of
X
, and a choice of grading in the sense of
[
Sei00
]
(the nullhomotopy of the Gauss map implies existence of a grading, distinguished up to even shift).

Theorem 1
.

There is a unital associative category
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
with objects spectral Lagrangian branes, whose morphism groups are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded modules over
\Omega^{fr}_{*}
, and with the property that isomorphic objects in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
represent the same class in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)
.

In Proposition
153
, we identify the endomorphisms of a spectral Lagrangian brane
L
with the framed bordism groups
\Omega_{*}^{fr}(L)
.

{rmk}

There is a forgetful (‘truncation’) functor
\tau_{\leq 0}:\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})\to H\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
with image the full subcategory of the integral Donaldson-Fukaya category of those exact Lagrangians which admit spectral brane structures.

{rmk}

In this paper we work with homological grading: under the truncation functor
\tau_{\leq 0}
of Remark
1.2
, degree
i
morphisms in
\tau_{\leq 0}\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
correspond to
CF^{-i}(L,K;\mathbb{Z})
.

A constraint in applying Theorem
1
is the difficulty in verifying isomorphism in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
.

The multiplication map

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S})\times\pi_{n-1}(\mathbb{S})\to\pi_{n}(\mathbb{S})\qquad\mathrm{equivalently}\qquad\Omega_{1}^{fr}\otimes\Omega_{n-1}^{fr}\to\Omega_{n}^{fr}

define a subgroup
\mathrm{image}(\eta)\subset\Omega_{n}^{fr}
(here
\eta
is the non-trivial element in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S})=\mathbb{Z}/2
). Since
\eta
is
2
-torsion, so is
\mathrm{image}(\eta)
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
X
be a Liouville manifold with
TX
stably trivial (as a complex vector bundle) of dimension
2n>6
, and fix a stable trivialisation
\phi
. Let
L,K\subset X
be closed exact Lagrangian integer homology spheres whose stable Gauss maps are stably trivial compatibly with
\phi
. If
L
and
K
represent quasi-isomorphic objects of
\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
up to shift, then they represent the same class in the quotient
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)/(\Omega_{1}^{fr}(X)\cdot\Omega^{fr}_{n-1})
. In particular,
[L]
and
[K]
agree in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}/\mathrm{image}(\eta)
.

We consider
\Omega_{n}^{fr;(n-1)\mathrm{-BS}}(X)\simeq\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)/(\Omega_{1}^{fr}(X)\cdot\Omega^{fr}_{n-1})
the group of bordism classes of stably framed manifolds with Baas-Sullivan singularities of dimension
n-1
mapping to
X
; see Section
6
for details.
To prove the theorem, we prove that
L
and
K
are isomorphic in
\tau_{\leq n}\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})_{(n-1)\mathrm{-BS}}
, a category which has the same objects as
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})
, but which only considers manifolds of dimension
\leq n+1
and allowing Baas-Sullivan singularities of dimension
n-1
.

{rmk}

The first constraints on the topology of Lagrangian submanifolds going beyond homotopy type were obtained by Abouzaid
[
Abo12a
]
, who showed that an exact Lagrangian homotopy sphere
L\subset T^{*}S^{4k+1}
framed bounds. Then
[
ES16
,
ES14
,
EKS16
]
showed that if homotopy
(2k+1)
-spheres
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
have
T^{*}\Sigma\simeq T^{*}\Sigma^{\prime}
then
[\Sigma]=\pm[\Sigma^{\prime}]\in\Theta_{2k+1}/bP_{2k}
. Using the Hopf correspondence
[
Tor
]
Torricelli showed there is a manifold
P\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{4}
which does not embed as a Lagrangian in
T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{4}
, and a ‘circle bundle construction’ shows that if
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{4k-1}\#\Sigma\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{4k-1}
is Lagrangian, then
\Sigma\#\Sigma
framed bounds. Crucially, the results in
[
Abo12a
,
ES16
]
rely on a displacing Hamiltonian isotopy for a suitable Lagrangian (
S^{2k+1}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{k+1}
respectively an immersed
S^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
), and build an explicit framed bounding manifold from the union of a moduli space of Floer holomorphic curves and an ‘artificial cap’ which closes off a boundary component of broken curves. The known generalisations have been largely tied to settings umbilically close to the cotangent bundle of an odd-dimensional sphere. The point of Theorem
2
is to provide a general statement for Weinstein manifolds in which a displacing isotopy is not required.

The following are examples of some of the applications proved in Section
2
.

{ex}

[
[
AAGCK
]
]
If
X=T^{*}S^{n}
and
L
is the zero-section then any
K
satisfies the hypotheses, and we conclude that
K\#K
framed bounds.

{ex}

If
X
is the
A_{2}
-plumbing
T^{*}S^{n}\#_{pt}T^{*}S^{n}
with
n=8
, we conclude that any exact
K
with vanishing Maslov class is necessarily diffeomorphic to
S^{8}
.

{ex}

Let
X
be the plumbing of
T^{*}S^{n}
and
T^{*}\Sigma
for a homotopy sphere
\Sigma
. Suppose
n
is even. If
\Sigma
does not have order 2 in the quotient
\Theta_{n}/bP_{n+1}
of the group of homotopy spheres by those that framed bound, then the spherical twist autoequivalence
T_{\Sigma}
of
\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
is not realised by any symplectomorphism.

{ex}

Attach a Weinstein four-handle to
T^{*}S^{7}
to obtain a Stein manifold
X
homotopy equivalent to
S^{7}\vee S^{4}
. A Lagrangian sphere
L\subset X
has a class in
\pi_{7}(S^{4})
whose image in
\pi_{3}^{st}=\mathbb{Z}/24
is two-torsion.

{ex}

Let
d\geq 3
and let
n
satisfy the constraints (a)
n\in\{0,4,6,7\}
mod
8
and (b) there is some element not of order 2 in
\Theta_{n+1}/bP_{n+2}
. If
X_{n,d}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
is the general affine hypersurface of degree
d
and complex dimension
n
then there is a non-trivial element of the symplectic mapping class group of
X
which acts trivially on the compact and wrapped Fukaya categories
\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
and
\scrW(X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Example
1.2
was independently established by Abouzaid, Alvarez-Gavela, Courte and Kragh
[
AAGCK
]
using methods more specifically tailored to working in cotangent bundles (generating functions and tube spaces). As far as we know Example
1.2
is new, and one of the first constraints on the smooth structure of an exact Lagrangian away from the case of cotangent bundles. Example
1.2
resolves a question raised by Seidel in
[
Sei14
]
. Quasi-isomorphic exact Lagrangians are known to be homologous (the argument is briefly recapped below), but the question of when quasi-isomorphic exact Lagrangian spheres are homotopic is more subtle; Example
1.2
gives a constraint in that direction.
Example
1.2
extends work of
[
DRE14
]
, and shows that ‘with positive probability’ (with respect to natural density) the symplectomorphism group of an affine hypersurface does not act faithfully on the Fukaya category.

Figure 1:
Framed bordism.

1.3
Motivation, techniques and a sketch of the argument

It is classical that if
L
and
K
are exact Lagrangian branes in an exact symplectic manifold
X
, and if
L
and
K
are quasi-isomorphic in
\scrF(X;R)
(for a commutative ring
R
, e.g.
R=\mathbb{Z}/2
or
R=\mathbb{Z}
if
L,K
are spin) then
L
and
K
represent the same homology class in
H_{n}(X;R)
.

A proof considers a moduli space of strips depicted in Figure
2
, where the morphisms
\alpha\in CF(L,K;R)
and
\beta\in CF(K,L;R)
are inverse quasi-isomorphisms i.e.
\alpha\cdot\beta=\id_{K}
and
\beta\cdot\alpha=\id_{L}
. Evaluation at the variable marked point along the dotted line defines a homology relation witnessing the equality
[L]=[K]\in H_{n}(X;R)
. The breakings coming from the evaluation marked point escaping to the boundary of the strip give boundary strata which, by exactness, involve moduli spaces of constant discs which (algebraically) sweep the fundamental class of the boundary component.

\alpha
\beta
K
L
\mathrm{ev}
CO(K)
CO(L)

Figure 2:
Homology relation from quasi-isomorphism and the length two open-closed map

More algebraically, the boundary strata on the right of the figure can be understood in terms of the open-closed map

\mathrm{OC}:CF^{*}(L,L)\to C_{*}(X)

which sends the unit to the fundamental class
[L]\in H_{n}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. Whilst the full open-closed map involves the
A_{\infty}
-structure on the Hochschild complex, the ‘length two’ version defines a map

\mathrm{Cone}\left(CF(L,K)\otimes CF(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}CF(L,L)\oplus CF(K,K)\right)\ \longrightarrow C^{*}(X),

where
\mu(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha\cdot\beta,\beta\cdot\alpha)
, which uses only homological information from the Donaldson-Fukaya category.

{rmk}

The previous argument is folklore and well-known to experts; a sketch is given for instance in
[
Li
, Section 3]
. There the homology relation defined by the universal family of holomorphic strips is interpreted as the existence of a ‘bordism current’ between the two fundamental classes.

A corollary of this fact is the following, which tells us that that the Fukaya category detects some bordism-theoretic information.

Corollary 3
.

In the situation outlined above with
R=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, then
L
and
K
have the same Pontrjagin numbers.

Proof 1.1
.

Since
TL\otimes\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to
TX|_{L}
, the Pontrjagin numbers of
L
are integrals of the form
\int_{L}p=\int_{L}c|_{L}
, where
p=p_{i_{1}}(L)\ldots p_{i_{k}}(L)\in H_{n}(L)
is a product of Pontrjagin classes of
L
and
c=c_{i_{1}}(X)\ldots c_{i_{k}}(X)\in H_{n}(X)
is the corresponding product of Chern classes. But this latter expression is equal to
[L]\cdot c
where
[L]\in H_{n}(X)
is the fundamental class; since
[L]=[K]
the result follows.

{rmk}

We recall that it is a classical fact that two closed manifolds are bordant if and only if they have the same Stiefel-Whitney numbers, and two oriented closed manifolds are oriented bordant if and only if they have the same Stiefel-Whitney and Pontrjagin numbers.

{rmk}

Dualising the argument arising from Figure
2
at chain level, and using the whole complex (rather than just focusing on the fundamental class), yields a homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{C^{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{*}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x\mapsto\alpha x\beta}
\textstyle{C^{*}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{*}(K)}

with the horizontal maps being the classical restrictions (by exactness of
L
and
K
). This shows that the distinguished isomorphism
\lambda:H^{*}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to H^{*}(K;\mathbb{Z})
arising from the Floer quasi-isomorphism identifies the Pontrjagin classes of
L
and
K
,
\lambda(p_{j}(L))=p_{j}(K)
. This strengthens Corollary
3
.

If
L
carries some further tangential structure, e.g. a stable framing, it is natural to wonder if Floer theory detects its class in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)
.

Suppose now
X
is a stably framed symplectic manifold and one has defined a spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category over the sphere spectrum
\mathbb{S}
with morphism groups
CF(L,K;\mathbb{S})
for spectral Lagrangian branes
L
and
K
. If
L
and
K
are integer homology spheres which are quasi-isomorphic in the usual integral Fukaya category, a version of the Whitehead theorem shows there is
\theta\in\pi_{n-1}(\mathbb{S})
with

CF(L,K;\mathbb{S})=\mathrm{Cone}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathbb{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{S}).

For instance,
if
L\pitchfork K=\{p,q\}
(transversely), the moduli space of holomorphic strips asymptotic to
p,q
, modulo the re-parametrization action of
\mathbb{R}
, is a compact
(n-1)
-manifold
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
. Vanishing of the stable Gauss maps of
L,K
imply that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
is stably framed. The Pontrjagin-Thom construction then associates to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
an element in the stable stem
\pi_{n-1}^{s}
, which is exactly
\theta
.

If
\theta=0
then
L
and
K
would be quasi-isomorphic over
\mathbb{S}
, and one would expect to infer equality of their framed bordism classes in
\pi_{n}(\mathbb{S})
. In particular, if one could pass to working over a ‘quotient spectrum’
R=\mathbb{S}/\pi_{n-1}(\mathbb{S})
then one would kill the obstruction, without necessarily killing all the information we’re trying to access (on the bordism classes of
L
and
K
, which live in a different degree). The main theorem in this paper realises this idea, working in a formalism of a spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category defined through bordism classes of flow modules, and with the role of the quotient spectrum
R
being played by bordism with Baas-Sullivan singularities.

{rmk}

There are structured categories of ring spectra which contain all colimits, and in which one can form a quotient such as
\mathbb{S}/\theta
for
\theta\in\pi_{n-1}(\mathbb{S})
. Since killing a class in stable homotopy will also kill its Whitehead self-products, the homotopy groups of this spectrum differ from those of
\mathbb{S}
not just in degree
n-1
but also in larger degrees, starting from degree
2(n-1)
. Since
2(n-1)>n
(the degree in which the bordism information we seek to constrain lies), this is not problematic for the heuristic sketch above. The same phenomenon appears in the setting of flow modules with Baas-Sullivan singularities modelled over cones on
k
-manifolds, where we require products of manifolds with such singularities to canonically carry the same structure. This relies on working with truncations of flow modules, keeping track of moduli spaces only up to some given dimension
n
, chosen so that
2k-1>n
, cf. Remark
6.1
.

{rmk}

Part of the ongoing work of Abouzaid and Blumberg, constructing foundations of Floer homotopy theory “without spectra”, has now appeared in
[
AB24
]
, after this paper was released. In particular, this includes a proof of a version of Conjecture
76
.

Our treatment of stable framings has also been influenced directly by their paper.

2
Applications

In this section, we provide several concrete applications of Theorem
2
to symplectic topology. In this section we only require the statement of Theorem
2
, and apart from that will not require any results from the rest of the paper, and in particular will not refer directly to any flow-categorical technology.

By a homology sphere, we always mean an integral homology sphere. Our conventions for the ‘ordinary’ Fukaya category follow those from
[
Sei08
]
.

2.1
Smooth structures on Lagrangian submanifolds

Lemma 4
.

If
\Sigma
is a homology sphere, then
T\Sigma
is stably trivial.

Proof 2.1
.

See
[
Ker69
, p.70]
.

It follows that
T^{*}\Sigma
admits a stable framing, so we can talk about the category
\scrF(T^{*}\Sigma;\mathbb{S})
. The zero-section
\Sigma
certainly forms one object of this category.

Lemma 5
.

If
\Sigma
is a homotopy sphere and
L\subset T^{*}\Sigma
is a closed exact Lagrangian submanifold, then
L
admits the structure of a spectral Lagrangian brane.

Proof 2.2
.

The stable Gauss map of
L
is zero on homotopy groups by
[
ACGK
, Theorem A]
.
L
is a homotopy sphere by
[
Abo12b
]
so this implies the stable Gauss map of
L
is nullhomotopic.

Now assume that
L\subset T^{*}\Sigma
is a closed exact Lagrangian, and either
\Sigma
is a homotopy sphere, or
\Sigma
is a homology sphere and
L
admits the structure of a Lagrangian brane.

Corollary 6
.

The class
[L]-[\Sigma]
in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(\ast)
lies in the image of multiplication by
\eta\in\Omega^{1}_{fr}(\ast)
.

Proof 2.3
.

L
and the zero-section
\Sigma
are quasi-isomorphic in
\scrF(T^{*}\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
by
[
FSS08
]
. The result then follows from Theorem
2
.

Since
\eta
is 2-torsion, we conclude that if
\Sigma=S^{n}
,
L\#L
framed bounds. For instance, this provides new constraints on exact Lagrangian submanifolds in
T^{*}S^{10}
. The group
\Theta_{10}/bP_{11}=\mathbb{Z}/6
is not 2-torsion, and four of the five exotic differentiable structures on the ten-dimensional topological sphere are ruled out from admitting an exact Lagrangian embedding.

We now consider the
A_{2}
-plumbing
A_{2}^{(n)}:=T^{*}S^{n}\#_{pt}T^{*}S^{n}
. This is also the affine hypersurface

\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{n}z_{i}^{2}+w^{3}=1\right\}\,\subset\,\mathbb{C}^{n+1}

equipped with the restriction of the standard symplectic form. There is a symplectic fibration over the space
\scrC
of cubic polynomials
p(w)
with distinct roots summing to zero, with fibre
\{\sum z_{i}^{2}+p(w)=0\}
over
p
.

Lemma 7
.

A_{2}^{(n)}
admits a polarisation for which the two core components admit the structure of spectral Lagrangian branes.

Proof 2.4
.

We follow an argument from
[
SS10
]
and
[
KS
]
. There is an exact sequence

T(A_{2}^{(n)})\to T\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to T\mathbb{C}

(1)

A (contractible) choice of Hermitian metric gives a splitting, which gives a stable trivialisation of the tangent space.

The Lagrangian core components
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
arises as vanishing cycles. More precisely there is a partial compactification
\overline{\scrC}
of
\scrC
of cubics with at most a double root, and an extension of the symplectic fibration over
\overline{\scrC}
with smooth total space and having nodal fibres over the discriminant (locus of cubics with a double root), see e.g.
[
KS02
,
SS10
]
. We can find a disc
D\subset\overline{\scrC}
normal to the discriminant and a one-parameter family
\scrX_{D}\to D
with fibre
A_{2}^{(n)}=X\mapsto 1
and such that
\scrX_{D}\supset\Delta_{a}
contains a Lagrangian thimble fibred over an arc
[0,1]\subset D
and with boundary
\Delta_{a}\cap X=S_{a}
.

Consider the following commutative diagram of vector bundles over
S_{a}
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces TS_{a}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces((T\Delta_{a})|_{S_{a}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{S_{a}\times\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TX|_{S_{a}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(\scrX_{D})|_{S_{a}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{a}\times\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(2)

Since
\Delta_{a}
is contractible, its tangent bundle
T\Delta_{a}
is trivial canonically up to homotopy, and that induces a stable trivialization of
TS_{a}
. One obtains a spectral brane structure on
S_{a}
by extending the stable Gauss map from
S_{a}
to the bounding thimble
\Delta_{a}
. The required compatibility, namely that the resulting trivialisation of
TS_{a}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=TX|_{S_{a}}
is homotopic to that arising from the trivialisation of
TX
fixed in (
1
), follows from the commutativity of (
2
).

The previous argument shows any matching sphere defines a spectral brane, since the stable polarisation of
A_{2}^{(n)}
is preserved by the natural action of the braid group.

Lemma 8
.

If
n\equiv\{0,4,6,7\}
mod
8
then any closed exact Lagrangian in
X
with Maslov class zero admits the structure of a spectral Lagrangian brane, which is quasiisomorphic in the Fukaya category over
\mathbb{Z}
to an actual Lagrangian sphere.

Proof 2.5
.

Under the Maslov class hypothesis,

L
is a homology sphere, which is quasi-isomorphic to an actual Lagrangian sphere
[
AS12
]
. The dimension hypothesis shows
\pi_{n}(U/O)=0
so the stable Gauss map of
L
(which depends only on its stable homotopy type) vanishes for degree reasons.

Then this, combined with Theorem
2
, proves:

Corollary 9
.

Under the previous conditions on
n
, if
L\subset A_{2}^{(n)}
is a closed exact Lagrangian with vanishing Maslov class, the class of
L
in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(\ast)
lies in the image of
\eta
, in particular is 2-torsion.

It follows that any exact Lagrangian in the 8-dimensional
A_{2}
-plumbing is diffeomorphic to
S^{8}
.

2.2
Exotic twists - non-existence

Consider the plumbing
X=T^{*}S^{n}\#_{pt}T^{*}\Sigma
of the cotangent bundles of the standard sphere
S^{n}
and an exotic sphere
\Sigma
; this is also the Weinstein manifold obtained by attaching a handle to the boundary of one fibre in
T^{*}\Sigma
. An application of our main result shows:

Theorem 10
.

If
n
is even and
\Sigma
does not have order 2 in
\Theta_{n}/bP_{n+1}
then the algebraic twist
T_{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{Auteq}(\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z}))
is not realised by any symplectomorphism.

This partially verifies a speculation of Seidel from
[
Sei14
, Section 5]
. We give a proof by contradiction, so suppose
\tau
is a symplectomorphism acting on the compact Fukaya category via the algebraic twist. For now
n
can be arbitrary, but we will need to constrain it in the course of the argument.

Let
L=S^{n}
denote the core component which is the standard sphere. By Lagrange surgery, there is an embedded exact Lagrangian
L\#\Sigma
in the homotopy class of the sum of the core components.

Lemma 11
.

In the integral Fukaya category
\scrF(X;\mathbb{Z})
, the objects
T_{\Sigma}(L)
and
L\#\Sigma
admit gradings with respect to which they are quasi-isomorphic.

Proof 2.6
.

There is a Lagrangian cobordism associated to the Polterovich surgery
[
Pol91
]
, giving an exact Lagrangian in
X\times\mathbb{C}
with ends carrying
L
,
\Sigma
and
L\#\Sigma
. It follows from
[
BC13
]
(who worked in the monotone case but use only an index-action relation on holomorphic discs which holds trivially in the exact case) that there is an exact triangle realising
L\#\Sigma
as a mapping cone
\mathrm{Cone}(L\to\Sigma)
of some morphism. Since
L\pitchfork\Sigma=\{p\}
is a single point, the possible morphisms are
\lambda p
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
. The cone is irreducible over any field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
because it has endomorphisms
H^{*}(L\#\Sigma;\mathbb{F}_{p})
, which shows that
\lambda\in\{\pm 1\}
. Adjusting the grading (and orientation) of
L
if necessary, the result follows.

Lemma 12
.

TX
admits a trivialisation, with respect to which the two core components
S^{n}
and
\Sigma
have nullhomotopic stable Gauss map.

Proof 2.7
.

We choose trivialisations of
TS^{n}
and
T\Sigma
, which, since
T(T^{*}S^{n})\simeq TS^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}
(and similarly for
\Sigma
) induce trivialisations of the tangent bundles of the two cotangent bundles, with respect to which the zero sections have nullhomotopic stable Gauss map.

The two trivialisations don’t agree on the nose over the plumbing region, but since the space of choices is contractible we may homotope them so that they do (without changing the fact that the zero-sections had nullhomotopic Gauss maps). We then can glue these trivialisations together over the plumbing region to get a trivialisation of
TX
.

Lemma 13
.

If
n
is even, the previously constructed polarisation is the unique stable polarisation of
X
, hence is preserved up to homotopy by
\tau
.

Proof 2.8
.

Two different stable polarisations differ by a map
X\to U
. Since
X
has the homotopy type of a wedge of even-dimensional spheres and
\pi_{even}(U)=0
the result follows.

It follows that
\tau
acts on the set of objects of the spectral Donaldson-Fukaya category. Since
\tau(L)
and
L
are diffeomorphic, they are certainly framed bordant, and since
L
is the standard sphere it follows that
\tau(L)
framed bounds. However, Theorem
2
implies that
\tau(L)
and
L\#\Sigma\simeq_{C^{\infty}}\Sigma
are quasi-isomorphic in
\scrF(X;\mathbb{S})_{\tau\leq n,(n-1)-\mathrm{BS}}
and hence have the same class in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}/\langle\eta\rangle
. It follows that
\Sigma\#\Sigma
framed bounds, i.e.
\Sigma
has order 2 in
\Theta_{n}/bP_{n+1}
.

2.3
Exotic twists - existence

Evans and Dmitroglou Rizell
[
DRE14
]
construct ‘exotic’ symplectomorphisms from Dehn twists defined with respect to non-standard parametrisations of the sphere. Their work relies on non-existence theorems for exact Lagrangian embeddings of certain homotopy spheres into cotangent bundles of the standard sphere, and gives rise to exotic symplectomorphisms of Stein manifolds which arise as fibres of a Lefschetz fibration on
T^{*}S^{k}
for appropriate
k
, in particular to the
A_{n}
Milnor fibres in dimension
k-1
. Theorem
2
allows one to extend their constructions to a wider and more natural class of affine varieties.

{hyp}

Let
X
be a Weinstein manifold which contains an
A_{2}
-chain of Lagrangian spheres
L,K
(so
L\pitchfork K=\{pt\}
). We assume that
X
admits a polarisation with respect to which each of
L
and
K
have spectral brane structures.

{ex}

We say that a smooth affine algebraic variety
X
admits an
A_{2}
-degeneration if there is a larger affine variety
\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
with general fibre
X
, with
0
-fibre having an isolated
A_{2}
-singularity, and with discriminant locally near
(0,0)
a cuspidal curve (with a nodal singularity over the smooth points). If
X
is an affine complete intersection, a variation on the argument from Section
2.1
, using that the vanishing cycles bound Lagrangian thimbles, shows that
X
satisfies the given hypotheses.

{ex}

The Milnor fibre of any isolated hypersurface singularity of Milnor number at least 2 admits an
A_{2}
-degeneration. The general smooth affine degree
d
hypersurface in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
admits an
A_{2}
-degeneration for
n\geq 2
and
d\geq 3
. (In fact the locus of hypersurfaces with an
A_{2}
-singularity is irreducible, so the
A_{2}
-configuration is essentially unique
[
Shi06
]
.)

For a diffeomorphism
\phi:S^{n}\to S^{n}
write

\tau_{L}^{\phi}=(d\phi)\circ\tau_{L}\circ(d\phi)^{-1}

which is still compactly supported near
L
, and let

\psi=\tau_{L}^{-1}\tau_{L}^{\phi}.

These are both compactly supported symplectomorphisms of
X
.

Lemma 14
.

\psi
preserves
K
setwise but changes its parametrization by
\phi
.

Proof 2.9
.

See
[
DRE14
, Lemma 3.5]
.

Let
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}
denote the Stein manifold which is the total space of a Lefschetz fibration with fibre
X
, with two critical fibres, both of which have vanishing cycle
K
. This contains a copy of
S^{n+1}
as a matching sphere.

Lemma 15
.

If
\psi\simeq\id
in
\Symp_{ct}(X)
then
\mathcal{X}
contains a Lagrangian submanifold
\Sigma(\phi)
which is the homotopy
(n+1)
-sphere defined by
\phi
.

Proof 2.10
.

See
[
DRE14
, Section 3.3 (proof of theorem A)]
; the argument uses the suspension of a Hamiltonian isotopy.

Lemma 16
.

In the situation of the previous Lemma, if
\psi\simeq\id
then the
\Sigma(\phi)
is quasi-isomorphic to the matching
S^{n+1}
in the Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof 2.11
.

From one of the main results of
[
Sei08
]
, the compact Fukaya category embeds in the Fukaya category of the Lefschetz fibration
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{S}(\pi)
so it is sufficient to prove quasi-isomorphism there. The category
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{S}(\pi)
is generated by the thimbles (for fields of characteristic not equal to two this is proved in
[
Sei08
]
, and over
\mathbb{Z}
it is proved in
[
GPS23
]
). We can assume that
S^{n+1}
is fibred over the upper half-circle and
\Sigma(\phi)
is fibred over a fattened arc which agrees with the lower half-circle near its end-points and is disjiont from
S^{n+1}
except at the end-points. This shows that both Lagrangians have
CF
of rank 1 with each of the two Lefschetz thimbles, so both are cones on a morphism
\Delta_{1}\to\Delta_{2}
. But this is a morphism from
K
to
K
in the Fukaya category of the fibre
X
, i.e. an element of
H^{*}(S^{n};\mathbb{Z})
. The morphism must be primitive because the cone is a homology sphere, and there is a unique morphism of the correct degree up to sign. Both Lagrangians admit canonical gradings which pins down the orientation and the sign.

The space
\mathcal{X}
is obtained from
X\times\mathbb{C}
by adding two
(n+1)
-handles along spheres with trivial Gauss map. If
\pi_{n+1}(U/O)=0
, which happens when
n\in\{3,5,6,7\}
modulo
8
, the stable polarisation extends, and the spheres
S^{n+1}
and
\Sigma(\phi)
are also automatically then polarised branes. The main theorem then implies that they define the same class in
\Omega_{n+1}^{fr}(\mathcal{X})/(\Omega^{fr}_{1}(\mathcal{X})\cdot\Omega^{fr}_{n})
.

Corollary 17
.

Under the previous conditions, if
\phi
does not have order 2, then
\psi
is not symplectically trivial.

Corollary 18
.

Let
X
be a generic affine hypersurface of degree
d\geq 3
and of dimension
n\in\{3,5,6,7\}
mod
8
. Assume that there is an element
\phi\in\Theta_{n}/bP_{n+1}
which is not 2-torsion. Then there is a compactly supported graded symplectomorphism which acts trivially on the integral Fukaya category.

{rmk}

For the
A_{k}
-Milnor fibre of complex dimension
n\geq 2
, it is known that the composite map

\pi_{1}\mathrm{Conf}_{k+1}(\mathbb{C})=Br_{k}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\Symp_{ct}(A_{k}^{(n)})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Auteq}(\scrF(A_{k}^{(n)};\mathbb{Z}))

is injective (working with the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded integral Fukaya category), and one infers from the existence of ‘exotically framed’ Dehn twists that in certain dimensions
n
the classical monodromy map

\pi_{1}\mathrm{Conf}_{k+1}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\Symp_{ct}(A_{k}^{(n)})

is not surjective. We similarly conjecture that, in the setting of Corollary
18
, the monodromy homomorphism is not onto the symplectic mapping class group. Note that the conditions on the degree and dimension suggest that this is a ‘typical’ phenomenon (happens with positive probability relative to natural density on the set of pairs
(d,n)
).

2.4
Homotopy classes of Lagrangian spheres

Quasi-isomorphic Lagrangian spheres are homologous. Are they homotopic? In general little is known.

Add a (subcritical!)
k
-handle to
T^{*}S^{n}
where
1<k<n
. The resulting Stein manifold
X
has homotopy type
S^{n}\vee S^{k}
. Assume that
k
and
n
both have modulo 8 reduction in
\{0,4,6,7\}
. Then the hypothesis on
k
implies that the stable polarisation of
T^{*}S^{n}
extends over the
k
-handle; the hypothesis on
n
implies that an exact Lagrangian sphere
L\subset X
also admits a stable polarisation.

Lemma 19
.

L
is quasi-isomorphic to the zero-section over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof 2.12
.

L
is a sphere so has vanishing Maslov class, hence defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded proper module over the wrapped category
\scrW(X)
. Since subcritical handle addition doesn’t change the wrapped category
[
GPS23
]
, the wrapped category of the cotangent bundle is generated by the fibre, and
HW(T_{x}^{*},T_{x}^{*})=\mathbb{Z}[x]
with
|x|=1-n
, we have that

\scrW(X)\simeq\scrW(T^{*}S^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[x])

A compact exact Lagrangian defines a proper module; the ascending degree filtration is a filtration by
A_{\infty}
-submodules, from which it follows that a Lagrangian sphere
L
in
X
is a twisted complex on the (image under
T^{*}S^{n}\subset X
of the) zero-section. The result then follows by (an easier version of) the homological algebra of
[
AS12
, Section 2]
, see also
[
AD
]
.

Theorem
2
then implies that the classes of
L
and the zero-section agree in
\Omega_{n}^{fr}(X)/(\Omega_{1}^{fr}\cdot\Omega_{n-1}^{fr})
.

Comparing the
(n+1)
-skeleton of
X
and the
(n+1)
-skeleton of
S^{k}\times S^{n}
shows that
\pi_{n}(X)=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\pi_{n}(S^{k})
. Projecting the fundamental class of
L
in
\Omega^{fr}_{n}(X)=\pi^{st}_{n}(X)
to the final factor gives a class in
\pi^{st}_{n-k}
; this is the same as the (stabilisation of the) projection of the homotopy class of
L
to a class in
\pi_{n}(S^{k})
. The previous conclusion then constrains the class of
[L]\in\pi_{n}(X)
in the image of the second factor in
\pi_{n-k}^{st}
:

Example 2.13
.

Adding a 4-handle to
T^{*}S^{7}
yields a manifold
X
for which any Lagrangian sphere
L
has a class in

\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/12=\pi_{7}(S^{4})\to\pi_{3}^{st}=\mathbb{Z}/24

and the image of the class on the RHS must be two-torsion.

3
Manifolds with faces

There is an extensive literature on manifolds with corners, with a number of different conventions and definitions, surveyed in
[
Joy12
, Remark 2.11]
, cf. also
[
Joy16
]
. We will largely deal with the special class typically called ‘manifolds with faces’.

3.1
Corners

Definition 20
.

A
manifold with corners

M
is a second-countable Hausdorff topological space equipped with an atlas of charts giving local homeomorphisms to open subsets of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
such that the transition functions are all smooth.

For a point
p
in
M
, write
\Gamma(p)=\Gamma^{M}(p)
for its
codimension
, meaning the codimension of the corner strata it lives in. This defines an upper semicontinuous function
\Gamma:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We write
M^{\circ}
for the interior of
M
, i.e.
M\setminus\partial M
. Equivalently this is the set of codimension 0 points in
M
.

A
manifold with faces
is a manifold with corners
M
such that the closure of any connected component of the set of points
p
in
M
of codimension
i
(for any
i
) is an embedded submanifold with corners.

A
face
of codimension
i
is a union of disjoint closures of connected components of the set of points
p
in
M
of codimension
i
. A
boundary face
will refer to a face of codimension 1.

Often we will say
F
is a face of
M
but
F
will not be defined to be a subset of
M
, in this case this means that we equip
F
with an embedding into
M
, so that its image is a face of
M
.

Definition 21
.

An embedding of manifolds with faces
f:M\rightarrow N
of codimension
i

strictly respects corner strata
if
\Gamma^{M}(p)=\Gamma^{N}(p)+i
for all
p
in
N
.

{ex}

The inclusion of any face
F
into
M
strictly respects corner strata.

{rmk}

Let
M^{n}
be a manifold with corners, and
p
a point in
M
. Then there is a chart near
p

\phi:U\subseteq M\rightarrow V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}
sending
p
to
0
, where
i=\Gamma(p)
is the codimension of
p
in
M
.

The following characterisation is often taken as the definition of a manifold with faces, see e.g.
[
J6̈8
]
.

Lemma 22
.

Let
M
be a manifold with corners. Then
M
is a manifold with faces if and only if for each point
p
in
M
,
p
lies in the closure of exactly
\Gamma(p)
boundary faces.

Proof 3.1
.

Note that
p
necessarily belongs to the closure of at most
\Gamma(p)
boundary faces, since it belongs to exactly
\Gamma(p)
components of the union of the boundary faces intersected with a sufficiently small local chart
p\in U\subset(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}
. If it belongs to strictly fewer than
\Gamma(p)
boundary faces then there must be some face whose closure is not embedded.

{ex}

The
n
-cube
[0,1]^{n}
is a manifold with faces for all
n\geq 0
.

The teardrop (two-dimensional disc with one corner) is a manifold with corners but not a manifold with faces.

Figure 3:
The teardrop

Lemma 23
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces. Then all of its faces are themselves manifolds with faces.

Proof 3.2
.

We first argue that any face
F
of codimension
i
of
M
is a manifold with corners.

Pick a point
p
in
F
. Pick a chart
\phi:U\subseteq M\rightarrow V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
, such that
U
and all its faces are connected, and
U
contains
p
. Then after reordering the coordinates
\phi(F)
must be of the form
U\cap\{0\}^{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-i}_{\geq 0}
. Identifying
\{0\}^{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-i}_{\geq 0}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n-i}_{\geq 0}
gives a chart on
F
as a manifold with corners. Allowing
p
to vary gives an atlas for
F
as a manifold with corners.

Let
G^{\circ}
be a component of the set of codimension
j
points in
F
. Then it is in particular a component of the set of codimension
i+j
points in
M
, so its closure is embedded in
M
and so also embedded in
F
.

Lemma 24
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces. If
F
is a face of
G
and
G
is a face of
M
, then
F
is a face of
M
.

Proof 3.3
.

Given a codimension
i
face
F
of
M
, the set of codimension
j
points in
F
is a union of components of the set of codimension
i+j
points in
M
, so the closure of any connected component is embedded in
M
and therefore also is embedded in
F
.

From this proof, we see that if
F
is a codimension
i
face of
M
, then the set of codimension
j
faces in
F
is the same as the set of codimension
i+j
faces in
M
which touch
F
.

Lemma 25
.

Let
M^{n}
be a manifold with faces, and let
F
and
G
be faces of
M
. Then their intersection is a union of faces of
M
.

Proof 3.4
.

We first show
F\cap G
is a manifold with corners. Let
F
be a codimension
i
face and
G
a codimension
j
face with
i\leq j
. To prove the lemma it suffices to treat the case in which
F
and
G
are connected. If
G\subset F
the result is obvious, so suppose
G\not\subset F
, and let
p
be a point of
F\cap G
. Then
p
is not in the interior of
G
, so we can replace
G
by a component of
\partial G
which replaces
(i,j)
by
(i,j+1)
. The proof is completed by downwards induction on
j-i
, where the maximal base case is where
G
is a point and
F
is (a connected component of) the whole space, and the result is clear.

More explicitly, pick a point
p
in
F\cap G
, and let
i=\Gamma^{M}(p)
. Pick a chart
\phi:U\subseteq M\rightarrow V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{i}_{\geq 0}
near
p
, such that
U
and all its faces are connected, and
\phi(p)=0
. Then after reordering the co-ordinates, we have that

\phi(F\cap U)=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}\,|\,y_{1}=\ldots=y_{a}=0

and

\phi(G\cap U)=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}\,|\,y_{a-c+1}=\ldots=y_{a-c+b}\right\}=0

where
a
is the codimension of
F
,
b
is the codimension of
G
and
c
is some other number. Therefore

\phi(F\cap G\cap U)=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}\,|\,y_{a-c+1}=\ldots=y_{a}=0\right\}.

This provides a chart for
F\cap G
as a manifold with corners of dimension
c
. Here
p
has codimension
i-c
in
F\cap G
. We see further from this local model that the closure of the smooth locus of
F\cap G
is an embedded submanifold with corners in
M
and therefore also in
F\cap G
.

Definition 26
.

A
system of boundary faces
for a manifold with corners
M
is a (finite) collection of embedded closed (as subsets) manifolds with corners
F_{i}\subseteq M
, lying in
\partial M
, all of codimension 1, such that each point
p
in
M
of codimension 1 lies in exactly one of the
F_{i}
.

A
system of faces
for a manifold with faces
M
is a collection of faces
F_{i}^{j}
of
M
such that each
F_{i}^{j}
has codimension
j
, and all
F_{i}^{j}
have disjoint smooth loci, and any (positive codimension) face of
M
is a disjoint union of components of the
F_{i}^{j}
. Equivalently, any point
p
in
M
of codimension
j>0
lives in the interior of exactly one of the
F_{i}^{j}
.

We will often say that
\{F_{i}^{j}\}_{i,j}
is a system of faces for
M
without the
F_{i}^{j}
being submanifolds of
M
; by this we mean that they are also equipped with embeddings
F_{i}^{j}\hookrightarrow M
making their images into a system of faces. Often some of the
F_{i}^{j}
will naturally be faces of each other, in which case the embeddings
F_{i}^{j}\hookrightarrow M
are assumed to be compatible with this structure.

Lemma 27
.

Let
M
be a manifold with corners, and
\{F_{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,k}
a system of boundary faces, which are all assumed to be manifolds with faces. Then
M
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by the intersections of the
F_{i}
, i.e. the

F_{I}:=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}F_{i}

Proof 3.5
.

Each
F_{I}
is a closed (as a subset) submanifold; it lies in some
F_{i}
which is a manfold with faces so
F_{I}
must be embedded in
M
. Therefore it suffices to show that each
p\in\partial M
lies in the interior of exactly one
F_{I}
.

Let
l=\Gamma(p)
. Then locally,
p
lies in the intersection of
l
of the boundary faces, say
F_{i_{1}},\ldots,F_{i_{l}}
. Letting
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\}
,
p
lies in the interior of
F_{I}
, and this is uniquely determined.

Lemma 28
.

Let
M
and
N
be manifolds with faces. Then
M\times N
is a manifold with faces, and its connected faces are exactly products of connected faces of
M
and
N
.

Proof 3.6
.

A connected component of the set of points
p
in
M\times N
of codimension
i
must be a product of such things in
M
and
N
(of codimensions
j
and
k
, such that
j+k=i
) respectively. Its closure is the product of the closures of the faces in
M
and
N
and since these are embedded this must therefore also be embedded.

3.2
Collars

Let
M
be a manifold with faces of dimension
n
. We write
D(M)
for the set of codimension 1 connected faces of
M
, and
D({F\subseteq M})
for the set of codimension 1 (connected) faces of
M
which touch
F
. This is a set with
\#D({F\subseteq M})=\Gamma(F)
.

If
F\subseteq M
is a boundary face, there is a natural map
D(F)\to D(M)\setminus\{F\}
, sending
F\cap G
to
G
for
G\in D(M)
.

In practice, when we list systems of faces, we will allow these sets to contain empty faces, and can therefore assume the map
D(F)\to D(M)\setminus\{F\}
is a bijection, for the sake of combinatorial sanity.

Definition 29
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces. A
system of normals

\nu
on
M
consists of non-zero inwards pointing sections
\nu^{F}
in
\gamma(TM|_{F})
for
F
in
D(M)
, such that for all
G
in
D({F\subseteq M})
,
\nu^{F}|_{F\cap G}
lies in the subspace
\gamma(TG|_{F\cap G})
of
\gamma(TM|_{F\cap G})
.

Since the space of systems of normals on a fixed
M
is convex, we have that

Lemma 30
.

The space of systems of normals on
M
is non-empty and convex, and hence contractible.

Lemma 31
.

Let
M
and
N
be manifolds with faces, equipped with systems of normals
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
respectively. Then there is a natural system of normals on
M\times N
.

Proof 3.7
.

Boundary one faces of
M\times N
are of the form
F\times N
or
M\times G
, for
F
and
G
boundary faces of
M
and
N
respectively. To each face
F\times N
we assign the normal vector field
(\nu^{F},0)
in
\Gamma(TM\oplus TN|_{F\times N})
, and similarly for the case
M\times G
.

Definition 32
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces. A
system of collars

\mathcal{C}
on
M
consists of collar neighbourhoods

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{FG}:F\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({F\subseteq G})}\hookrightarrow G

meaning an embedding onto a neighbourhood of
F\subseteq G
, whenever
F
and
G
are faces of
M
of codimension
i
and
j
respectively, such that
F\subseteq G
. We require that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{F\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({F\subseteq M})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{F\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({F\subseteq G})}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({G\subseteq M})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{G\times[0,\varepsilon)^{D({G\subseteq M})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{M}

There is an equivalent inductive definition. We could define a
system of collars
on
M
to consist of systems of collars on all positive codimension faces in
M
, which restrict to each other on subfaces, along with maps

\mathcal{C}:F\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M

for all boundary faces
F
such that for all distinct pairs of boundary faces
F_{i},F_{j}
, the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{F_{i}\cap F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}^{F_{i}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\mathcal{C}^{F_{j}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}\right)\circ\tau}
\textstyle{F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{M}

(3)

where
\tau
swaps the two factors in
[0,\varepsilon)^{2}
.

Definition 33
.

By taking the derivative of
\mathcal{C}^{F}
in the inwards normal direction for each boundary face
F
, a system of collars on
M
determines a system of normals on
M
,
\nu(\mathcal{C})
. We say a system of collars
\mathcal{C}

extends
a system of normals
\nu
if
\nu(\mathcal{C})=\nu
. If this holds, we can extend
\nu^{F}
to a section of
TM
over the whole of
\im\mathcal{C}^{F}
by taking the derivative of
\mathcal{C}^{F}
in the inwards normal direction.

Lemma 34
.

Let
M
and
N
be manifolds with faces, equipped with systems of collars
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
respectively. Then there is a natural system of collars on
M\times N
.

Proof 3.8
.

We define a system of corners
\mathcal{E}
on
M\times N
. Let
F\subseteq F^{\prime}
be faces of
M
of codimension
i
and
i^{\prime}
respectively, and
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
be faces of
N
of codimensions
j
and
j^{\prime}
respectively. Note that
F\times G
is a face in
F^{\prime}\times G^{\prime}
of codimension
i-i^{\prime}+j-j^{\prime}
. Then we define

\mathcal{E}:F\times G\times[0,\varepsilon)^{i-i^{\prime}+j-j^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow F^{\prime}\times G^{\prime}

to be
\mathcal{C}_{FF^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{D}_{GG^{\prime}}
.

We will use extensively the following version of the collar neighbourhood theorem for manifolds with faces.

Lemma 35
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces, with a system of boundary faces
F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}
. Assume all the boundary faces
F_{i}
are equipped with systems of faces which agree on their overlaps. Then there exists a system of collars on
M
extending those on the
F_{i}
. Furthermore this is unique up to isotopy of such systems of collars, and in particular is unique up to an ambient diffeomorphism of
M
which is the identity on
\partial M
.

For a fixed system of normals
\nu
, existence and uniqueness still holds if we require that the systems of collars (and isotopy) all extend
\nu
.

Proof 3.9
.

We use the second definition of a system of collars, and proceed inductively. We first prove existence.

We build the collar neighbourhoods we need by induction. We first assume we have collar neighbourhoods
\mathcal{C}_{i}:F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M
such that Square
3
commutes for
i,j\leq l-1
. We will build a collar neighbourhood
\mathcal{C}_{l}:F_{l}\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M
so that Square
3
commutes for
i,j\leq l
. Then by induction on
l
this will complete the proof.

We first choose any collar neighbourhood
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{l}:F_{l}\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M
. We induct again, and assume Square
3
commutes for
i\leq m-1,j=l
, and we will isotope
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{l}
without breaking this, so that Square
3
commutes for
i=m,j=l
too. Then by induction on
m
this will complete the proof.

We consider Square
3
for
i=m,j=l
. This doesn’t necessarily commute, but by uniqueness of collar neighbourhoods it does up to some isotopy
\Phi
, which we can extend to an isotopy of the whole collar
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{l}
. However it does commute in a neighbourhood of
F_{i}\cap F_{m}\cap F_{l}
since we know that Square
3
commutes, for all
i\leq m-1
. Therefore we can choose the isotopy
\Phi
to be constant in a neighbourhood of all
F_{i}\cap F_{m}\cap F_{l}
for all
i\leq m-1
. Then the other end of the isotopy provides a collar
\mathcal{C}_{l}
such that Square
3
commutes for
i\leq m,j\leq l
.

We next prove uniqueness. Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two systems of collars on
M
extending the given ones on the
F_{i}
. We wish to build smooth 1-parameter families of embeddings

\{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}:F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M

onto a neighbourhood of
F_{i}
for all
i
, such that
\mathcal{I}^{0}_{i}=\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i}
and such that the following analogue of Square
3
commutes for all
t
,
i
:

\textstyle{F_{i}\cap F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}^{F_{i}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\mathcal{C}^{F_{j}}\times\id_{[0,\varepsilon)}\right)\circ\tau}
\textstyle{F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{i}^{t}}
\textstyle{F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{j}^{t}}
\textstyle{M}

(4)

We proceed inductively as before. Assume we’ve build
\mathcal{I}_{i}^{t}
such that Square
4
commutes for
i,j\leq l-1
. We then build an isotopy
\mathcal{I}_{i}^{t}
so that Square
4
commutes for
i,j\leq l
, then conclude by induction on
l
.

We first choose any isotopy
\mathcal{I}^{\prime t}_{l}:F_{l}\times[0,\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow M
between
\mathcal{C}_{l}
and
\mathcal{D}_{l}
, using the uniqueness of collar neighbourhoods. We induct again, and assume Square
4
commutes for
i\leq m-1
,
j=l
, and we will isotope
\mathcal{I}^{\prime t}_{l}
(through isotopies from
\mathcal{C}_{i}
to
\mathcal{D}_{i}
) without breaking this, so that Square
4
commutes for
i=m
,
j=l
too. Then conclude by induction on
m
.

We consider Square
4
for
i=m
,
j=l
. This commutes for
t=0
and
t=1
but not arbitrary
t
, but by uniqueness of collar neighbourhoods in families it does up to some
t
-dependent isotopy
\Phi^{t}
, which is the identity for
t=0
and
t=1
. We extend this to an isotopy of the whole isotopy
\mathcal{I}_{l}^{t}
. However since Square
4
commuted in a neighbourhood of all
F_{i}\cap F_{m}\cap F_{l}
for
i\leq m-1
, we can choose
\Phi^{t}
to be constant in a neighbourhood of all
F_{i}\cap F_{m}\cap F_{l}
for
i\leq m-1
. Then the other end of the isotopy
\Phi
provides the isotopy
\mathcal{I}_{l}^{t}
such that Square
4
commutes for
i\leq m
,
j\leq l
.

The entirety of the above proof can be performed the same with a fixed system of normals
\nu
if we require that all collar neighbourhoods (and isotopies) extend
\nu
.

Corollary 36
.

Let
M
be a manifold with corners, and assume some of its faces are equipped with systems of collars. Then
M
admits a system of collars extending them, and this is unique up to isotopy of such things, and in particular is unique up to diffeomorphisms of
M
which are the identity on the faces which were already equipped with systems of collars.

3.3
Abstract gluing

Let
M
be a manifold with faces, equipped with a system of collars. We can describe a neighbourhood of
\partial M
, as a manifold with corners, in terms of the corner strata of
M
. Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}
be a system of boundary faces for
M
. Define

\tilde{M}:=\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{i}F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\right)/\sim

where we define
\sim
by
(\mathcal{C}^{F_{i}\cap F_{j},F_{i}}(x,s),t)\sim(\mathcal{C}^{F_{i}\cap F_{j},F_{j}}(x,t),s)
for
x
in
F_{i}\cap F_{j}
(
j\neq i
here), and
s,t
in
[0,\varepsilon)
. This equivalence relation is defined so that as a topological space,
M
is the coequaliser of the two maps defined above

\bigsqcup\limits_{i<j}F_{i}\cap F_{j}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup\limits_{i}F_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon).

Then the maps
\mathcal{C}
define an embedding
\tilde{M}\hookrightarrow M
onto a neighbourhood of the boundary.

It follows from the fact that
\tilde{M}
is an open subset in
M
that
\tilde{M}
is a manifold with faces, but as we see in Lemma
37
, we can prove this without reference to the ambient manifold
M
.

In Section
4
, there will be situations where we would like (but don’t yet have)
M
, but we do have the data of its boundary faces along with the data of how they overlap; we then form
\tilde{M}
using Lemma
37
and then use this as a first step towards bulding
M
.

Lemma 37
.

Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a finite collection of manifolds with faces.

Let
\{N_{w}\}_{w\in W}
be a finite collection of manifolds with faces, equipped with inclusion maps

\textstyle{N_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{w}}
\textstyle{M_{i_{w}}}
\textstyle{M_{j_{w}}}

each of which is a diffeomorphism onto a boundary face, and such that no boundary face is the image of two of these maps.

Define

Z:=\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{i}M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\right)/\sim

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined by

(\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{w}(x),u),v)\sim(\mathcal{C}(\beta_{w}(x),v),u)

for
x
in any
N_{w}
and
(u,v)
in
[0,\varepsilon)^{2}_{u,v}
.

Equivalently,
Z
is the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\bigsqcup\limits_{w}N_{w}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}_{u,v}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup_{i}M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)_{s}

where the two maps
A
and
B
send
(x,u,v)
to
(\mathcal{C}(\alpha_{w}(x),u),v)
and
(\mathcal{C}(\beta_{w}(x),v),u)
respectively.

Then
Z
is a manifold with corners.

In the cases where we apply this,
Z
will generally be a manifold with faces, but we will verify this separately (and describe a system of faces) in each case.

For a manifold
Z
constructed as above, we write
C(Z)
for the subspace
\cup_{i}M_{i}\times\{0\}
. This is itself not a manifold, but is a homotopy-equivalent subspace of
Z
. We call this the
carapace
of
Z
.

Proof 3.10
.

First note that since no boundary face of any
M_{i}
is the image of more than one of the
\alpha_{w}
or
\beta_{w}
, the quotient map

\bigsqcup\limits_{i}M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)\to Z

has fibres which are all discrete and are either one or two points, and the restriction to each
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
is an embedding of topological spaces.

We first show that
Z
is Hausdorff. Indeed suppose
\{p_{n}\}_{n}
is a sequence which has two limits
(x,s)
and
(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})
in
Z
. Here
s
and
s^{\prime}
lie in
[0,\varepsilon)
,
x
lies in
M_{i}
and
x^{\prime}
lies in
M_{i^{\prime}}
.

If
i=i^{\prime}
, i.e.
x
and
x^{\prime}
both lie in
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
, then the sequence
p_{n}
would also eventually lie in the image of
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
in
Z
, but because
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
is itself Hausdorff, and no two points in
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
are identified with each other under
\sim
, the image of
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
in
Z
is Hausdorff.

We now assume
i\neq i^{\prime}
. Then for sufficiently large
n
,
p_{n}
must lie in both
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
and
M_{i^{\prime}}\times[0,\varepsilon)
(since they’re open neighbourhoods of
x
and
x^{\prime}
respectively) (and can live in no other
M_{j}
with
j\neq i,i^{\prime}
), and so must be of the form
(a_{n},s_{n})
for
a_{n}
in
M_{i}
and
s_{n}
in
[0,\varepsilon)
, but
p_{n}
must also be of the form
(b_{n},t_{n})
for
b_{n}
in
M_{i^{\prime}}
and
t_{n}
in
[0,\varepsilon)
.

Since there are only finitely many
N_{w}
, restricting to a subsequence if necessary,
p_{n}
must therefore be of the form
(c_{n},u_{n},v_{n})
, where
c_{n}
lies in some
N_{w}
, where
i_{w}=i
and
j_{w}=i^{\prime}
.
x
and
x^{\prime}
can’t lie in the image of
N_{w}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}
in
Z
as otherwise they would both lie in
M_{i}
. For these to converge to points in
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
and
M_{i^{\prime}}\times[0,\varepsilon)
not in
N_{w}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}
, we must have that
u_{n}
and
v_{n}
both converge to
\varepsilon
. But such a sequence has no limit in
Z
.

Then since
A
and
B
are smooth embeddings of manifolds with faces which strictly respect corner strata, since it is Hausdorff,
Z
will be a manifold with corners.

We will need conditions under which this
Z
is a manifold with faces, and a description of a system of faces in such a case. We work in the setting of Lemma
37
.

Lemma 38
.

Assume we are given a finite set
J
and that
W=\{(i,i^{\prime})\in I\times I\,|\,i\neq i^{\prime}\}
,
i_{(i,i^{\prime})}=i
and
j_{(i,i^{\prime})}=i^{\prime}
.

Assume we are also given bijections
\lambda_{i}:I\setminus\{i\}\sqcup J\to D(M_{i})
for all
i
, and bijections
\lambda_{ii^{\prime}}:I\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}\sqcup J\to D(N_{(i,i^{\prime})})
such that the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{I\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}\sqcup J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{ii^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{I\setminus\{i\}\sqcup J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{i}}
\textstyle{D(N_{(i,i^{\prime})})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D(M_{i})}

and

\textstyle{I\setminus\{i,i^{\prime}\}\sqcup J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{ii^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{I\setminus\{i^{\prime}\}\sqcup J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{i^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{D(N_{(i,i^{\prime})})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D(M_{i^{\prime}})}

where the top horizontal maps are the natural inclusions, and the bottom horizonal maps are those induced by the inclusions
\alpha_{(i,i^{\prime})}
and
\beta_{(i,i^{\prime})}
respectively.

For
K\subseteq J
, write
M_{i}^{K},N^{K}_{(i,i^{\prime})}
for the faces in
M_{i}
and
N_{(i,i^{\prime})}
respectively, which are the intersections of the boundary faces corresponding to the subsets
\lambda_{i}(K)
and
\lambda_{ii^{\prime}}(K)
of
D(M_{i})
and
D(N_{(i,i^{\prime})})
respectively. Define
Z^{K}
to be the coequaliser of the following maps

\bigsqcup\limits_{(i,i^{\prime})\in W}N_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{K}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup_{i}M_{i}^{K}\times[0,\varepsilon)

where the two maps are defined by the same formulas as in Lemma
37
.

Then each
Z^{K}
is a manifold with corners by Lemma
37
, and in fact is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

Z^{K^{\prime}}

for
K\subseteq K^{\prime}\subseteq J
, and

M_{I^{\prime}}^{K^{\prime}}\times\{0\}

for for
K\subseteq K^{\prime}\subseteq J
and
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, where
M_{I^{\prime}}\times\{0\}
is defined to be the intersection of all
M_{i}\times\{0\}
for
i\in I^{\prime}
.

In particular,
Z^{K}
has a system of boundary faces given by

Z^{\{j\}\cup K}

for
i\in I
and
j\in J\setminus K
, and

M_{i}^{K}\times\{0\}

Note that in particular, the faces of
Z^{K}
of the form
Z^{K^{\prime}}
are non-compact, whereas those of the form
M^{K^{\prime}}_{I^{\prime}}\times\{0\}
are compact.

Proof 3.11
.

It suffices to prove this in the case
K=\emptyset
, and since each of the list of faces given is indeed the intersection of some subset of the given list of boundary faces, it suffices to show the given list of boundary faces is indeed a system of boundary faces.

Let
p\in Z^{\emptyset}
be a point of codimension 1. Then
p=(x,t)
lies in some
M_{i}\times[0,\varepsilon)
. If
t=0
,
x
must lie in the interior of
M_{i}
(since
\Gamma(p)=1
), so
p
lies in the interior of
M_{i}\times\{0\}
.

If
t\neq 0
,
p
lies in the interior of some boundary face
F
of
M_{i}
. Then
p
lies in the interior of
Z^{\{F\}}
.

3.4
Smooth functions on manifolds with faces

Definition 39
.

Let
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth map, where
M
is a manifold with faces. We define a regular value of
f
inductively in the dimension. We say
p\in\mathbb{R}
is a
regular value
of
f
if
df_{x}:T_{x}M\rightarrow T_{p}\mathbb{R}
is surjective for all
x\in f^{-1}\{p\}
, and the restrictions of
f
to all faces of
M
have
p
as a regular value.

Sard’s theorem implies that for a fixed
f
, this condition is satisfied for generic
p
. We also have a version of the preimage theorem for manifolds with faces:

Lemma 40
.

Let
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth map, where
M
is a manifold with faces and
p\in\mathbb{R}
a regular value. Then
f^{-1}\{p\}
and
f^{-1}(-\infty,p]
are naturally smooth manifolds with faces.

Let
F
be a face of
M
. Then
F\cap f^{-1}\{p\}
is a face of
f^{-1}\{p\}
, and any face of
f^{-1}\{p\}
is a union of components of such things.

Similarly,
F\cap f^{-1}\{p\}
and
F\cap f^{-1}(-\infty,p]
are faces of
f^{-1}(-\infty,p]
, and any face of
f^{-1}(-\infty,p]
is a union of components of such things.

Proof 3.12
.

Write
M=\cup_{i}M_{i}
where
M_{i}
denotes the set of points of codimension at most
i
, so
M_{0}=M^{\circ}
. We proceed by induction on
i
, with the case
i=1
being the usual preimage theorem for manifolds with boundary. Let
p
be a regular value of
f
; we assume that
f^{-1}(p)\cap M_{i-1}
is a smooth manifold with corners, whose corner strata are given by the complements
f^{-1}(p)\cap M_{j}\backslash M_{j-1}
for
j<i
. Now let
x\in f^{-1}(p)\cap M_{i}
, and pick a chart

\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times[0,\infty)^{i}\supset U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}M

near
x
, and translate in the range so
p=0
. By smoothness of
f
, we can find
g
an extension of
f\circ\iota
to a neighbourhood of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so that
g^{-1}(0)=W\subset U
is locally a smooth hypersurface (without boundary) near
x
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n-i+1}\times[0,\infty)^{i+1}
is a smooth manifold with the expected corner strata. Write
pr_{j}
for the
j
-th co-ordinate projection on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note

W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\times[0,\infty)^{i}=\left\{w\in W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n-i+1}\times[0,\infty)^{i+1}\,|\,pr_{j}(w)\geq 0\,\forall n-i+1\leq j\leq n\right\}.

Pick a point
w
where the projection
pr_{j}
vanishes for at most
i-1
indices
j\in\{n-i+1,\ldots,n\}
and let
j_{0}
denote the exceptional index. It suffices to prove that
0
is a regular value of
pr_{j_{0}}
. But if not, then
T_{w}W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}=\{j_{0}=0\}
and then a dimension count shows that
\ker(dg|_{w})
has dimension at most
n-2-i
, which violates the hypothesis of regularity of
f
on the codimension
i
locus.

It follows by induction that
f^{-1}(p)
is a manifold with corners, whose corners are given by the level sets of
f
on the corner strata of
M
. Since embeddedness of closures of components is inherited from
M
, it follows that
f^{-1}(p)
is a manifold with faces, and its faces are as claimed. The result for
f^{-1}(-\infty,p]
is analogous.

Lemma 41
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces and
\{F_{i}\}
a collection of faces of any codimension in
M
(we do not assume they form a system of faces of
M
or even that they cover the whole of
\partial M
). Assume we have smooth maps
f_{i}:F_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which agree on the overlaps of the
F_{i}
in
M
. Then there is a smooth map
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which restricts to
f_{i}
on each
F_{i}
.

If all the
f_{i}
are proper over
(-\infty,1+2\delta]
for some
\delta>0
, then an extension
f
can be chosen which is proper over
(-\infty,1+\delta]
; similarly if the
F_{i}
are proper over
(-\infty,1+2\delta]
and also bounded below.

Proof 3.13
.

By induction, we may assume
M
is equipped with a system of faces and we are given smooth maps
F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for all faces
F
, which agree on their overlaps. By using a partition of unity, it then suffices to construct the extension
f
in the case when
M=\mathbb{R}^{n-j}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{j}
.

The existence of some smooth extension can be given directly, for instance, one can take

f(x,y):=\sum\limits_{1\leq k_{1}<\ldots<k_{l}\leq j}(-1)^{1+l}f_{k_{1}}(x,\bar{y})

where in each term,
\bar{y}
is
y
with the
k_{1}^{\mathrm{th}},\ldots,k_{l}^{\mathrm{th}}
entries set to 0.

We now establish the claim on properness.
Let
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
be a global extension of the
\{f_{i}\}
, so
f:\partial M\to\mathbb{R}
is proper over the given interval
(-\infty,1+2\delta]
. Pick a metric on
M
, with distance function
d
, and let

U=\{x\in M\,|\,d(x,\partial M)<\delta\}

which is an open neighbourhood of the boundary
\partial M
. We further pick a retraction

r:Cl(U)\to\partial M

with the property that
d(x,r(x))<\delta^{\prime}
for
x\in Cl(U)
, where
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
is determined by the Lipschitz constant of the retraction
r
. We now let

U\supset U^{\prime}=\{x\in U\,|\,d(f(x),f(rx))<\delta/2\}

which is a smaller open neighbourhood of
\partial M
.
Take a partition of unity
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
subordinate to the open cover
U^{\prime},M^{\circ}
of
M
, where
M^{\circ}
denotes the interior (complement of all boundary faces). We consider the function

G(x)=\lambda(x)f(x)+\lambda^{\prime}(x)(1+2\delta)

which also restricts to
f
on
\partial M
.
For
[a,b]\subset G^{-1}(-\infty,1+\delta])
consider
G^{-1}[a,b]
. If
x\not\in Cl(U^{\prime})
then
\lambda^{\prime}(x)=1
and
\lambda(x)=0
so
G(x)>1+\delta
, so
G^{-1}[a,b]\subset U^{\prime}\subset U
.

Now suppose we have a sequence
x_{n}\in G^{-1}[a,b]
which does not converge. The condition that
d(f(x_{n}),f(r(x_{n})))<\delta/2
shows that
r(x_{n})\in f^{-1}[a-\delta/2,b+\delta/2]
. This is a compact subset of
\partial M
by properness of
f|_{\partial M}
over
(-\infty,1+2\delta]
. Therefore
r(x_{n})
have some limit
w
. If
d(x_{n},x_{0})\to\infty
then
d(x_{n},r(x_{n}))
will also diverge to infinity, since
r(x_{n})
converge in
\partial M
, a contradiction. It follows that eventually the
x_{n}
lie in a closed ball inside
U^{\prime}
and hence have a convergent subsequence. This establishes properness of the extension
G
over
(-\infty,1+\delta]
. If the
F_{i}
are in addition bounded below then so is the constructed
G
.

{rmk}

In the situation of the previous Lemma, if
1
is a regular value of all the
F_{i}
, then we may construct the extension
f
(proper over an interval and bounded below as appropriate) so that
1
is still a regular value.

3.5
Framed functions

In this section, we consider smooth maps
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
equipped with some extra data, that will be useful when we consider stable framings.

Definition 42
.

Let
M
be a (possibly non-compact) manifold with faces and a system of collars. A
framed function
is a pair
(f,s)
, where
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth map with 1 as a regular value and
s:\mathbb{R}\to TM|_{f^{-1}\{1\}}
is a map of vector bundles over
f^{-1}\{1\}
, satisfying

1.

f
is negative on compact faces of
M
.

2.

f^{-1}(-\infty,2]
is compact.

3.

df
vanishes on each inwards-pointing normal vector along each codimension 1 face.

4.

Over each codimension 1 face
F
,
s
lives inside this face, i.e.
s|_{F}
factors through
TF\subseteq TM|_{F}
.

5.

The composition

\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{s}TM|_{f^{-1}\{1\}}\xrightarrow{df}\mathbb{R}

is the identity.

The final condition implies that a framed function determines an isomorphism of vector bundles, which we call
S=di+s:Tf^{-1}\{1\}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to TM|_{f^{-1}\{1\}}
, where
i:f^{-1}\{1\}\to M
is the inclusion.

Proposition 43
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces and a system of collars, and
(f,s)
a framed function on
M
. Then the restriction of
(f,s)
to a face
F
of
M
is a framed function.

Furthermore
f^{-1}\{1\}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by the set of
f|_{F}^{-1}\{1\}
for
F
a non-compact face of
M
, and
f^{-1}\{1\}
inherits a natural system of collars.

The isomorphisms
S
are compatible with restriction to the boundary, meaning for each boundary face
F
of
M
, there is a commutative diagram of isomorphisms of vector bundles over
\im\mathcal{C}^{F}
:

\textstyle{Tf|_{F}^{-1}\{1\}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\nu^{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{TF\oplus\mathbb{R}\nu^{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Tf^{-1}\{1\}\oplus\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\textstyle{TM}

Proof 3.14
.

We first show that
(f,s)
restricts to a face
F
as a framed function. Since every compact face of
F
is a compact face of
M
, the first two conditions in the definition are immediate.
Let
G\subset F
be a codimension one face of
F
with inwards-pointing normal
\nu
. Then there is some codimension one face
\hat{G}\subset M
containing
F
with inwards normal
\nu
. It follows that
d(f|_{F})(\nu)=0
, which establishes the third condition. The fourth condition similarly follows from the fact that any codimension one face
G\subset F
is an intersection
F\cap\hat{G}
of
F
with another codimension one face
\hat{G}\subset M
, and that
s|_{\hat{G}}
factors through
T\hat{G}
. The final condition
df\circ s=\id
restricts to give the same identity and hence splitting on
TF|_{f^{-1}(1)}
.

The second paragraph holds by Lemma
40
, and the diagram is then immediate from the construction.

Proposition 44
.

Let
M
be a manifold with faces and a system of collars. Assume each boundary face
F
of
M
is equipped with a framed function
(f^{F},s^{F})
, so that these agree on the overlaps between different boundary faces.

Then there exists a framed function
(f,s)
on
M
restricting to
(f^{F},s^{F})
over each boundary face
F
of
M
.

Proof 3.15
.

We first choose an extension
f
of each
f^{F}
, which we can do by Lemma
41
; postcomposing with an appropriate automorphism of
\mathbb{R}
if necessary to make it proper over
f^{-1}(-\infty,2]
. Generically perturbing if necessary we may assume
f
has 1 as a regular value.

For fixed
f
, the space of bundle maps
s:\mathbb{R}\to TM|_{f^{-1}\{1\}}
satisfying the appropriate conditions is defined by a local condition, which is also a convex condition, so there exists an appropriate extension by a standard partition of unity argument.

3.6
Stable isomorphisms

In this section, we fix some conventions regarding stable vector bundles and isomorphism classes between them; in particular we do not work up to homotopy here. We implicitly assume all vector spaces and bundles we encounter are equipped with fibrewise inner products, compatible with restriction and direct sum decompositions; such choices always exist.

Definition 45
.

Let
E=E^{+}-E^{-}
and
F=F^{+}-F^{-}
be stable vector bundles over a compact base
B
(so
E^{\pm},F^{\pm}
are both actual vector bundles). A
stable isomorphism
between
E
and
F
is a pair
(S,\psi)
, where
S\to B
is a vector bundle, and
\psi
is an isomorphism of vector bundles

\psi:E^{+}\oplus F^{-}\oplus S\to F^{+}\oplus E^{-}\oplus S

We often write this as

\psi:E\to F

We call
S
the
stabilising bundle
.

Given a stable isomorphism
(S,\psi)
and an embedding
\iota:S\hookrightarrow T
, we obtain another stable isomorphism
(T,\psi^{\prime})
, by setting
\psi^{\prime}
to be the identity on the orthogonal complement of
S
inside
T
, and to be
\psi
on the rest; we call the resulting stable isomorphism the
extension
of
(S,\psi)

to

T
or
along

\iota
.

Given stable isomorphisms
(S,\psi):E\to F
and
(T,\phi):F\to G
, there is a stable isomorphism
(S\oplus F^{-}\oplus T,\theta)
, where
\theta
is given by
\phi
composed with
\psi
, acting on the appropriate summands. Composition of stable isomorphisms is then associative, though see Remark
3.6
.

{rmk}

In the usual model for the category of vector spaces it is not true that the direct sum is strictly associative (meaning
A\oplus(B\oplus C)
and
(A\oplus B)\oplus C
aren’t equal, they are instead related by a canonical associator isomorphism). There are two ways around this issue; we briefly sketch them both here, and the reader may choose which to apply.

1.

Following
[
Isb69
]
(see also
[
LLS23
, Section 2.8]
), one may work with a category equivalent to the category of vector spaces which is equipped with a strictly associative direct sum operation.

2.

One may keep track of all these canonical associator isomorphisms; in many cases when we compare two stable isomorphisms, we do not require that they are equal on the nose, but instead require that they agree after applying appropriate associator isomorphisms.

We may apply the same definition to stable vector spaces, which we think of as stable vector bundles over a point.

{rmk}

Given stable isomorphisms
(S,\psi):E\oplus V\to F
and
(T,\phi):F\oplus W\to G
, there are now two natural stable isomorphisms
E\oplus V\oplus W\to G
.

The first is the following composition of stable isomorphisms:

E\oplus V\oplus W\xrightarrow{\psi}F\oplus W\xrightarrow{\phi}G

constructed as above, and in particular has stabilising bundle
(S\oplus F^{-}\oplus T)
.

The second is the vector bundle isomorphism
\psi\oplus\phi
with stabilising bundle
S\oplus F^{+}\oplus F^{-}\oplus T
.

When working with flow categories we will use the first construction, but when equipping the product of stably framed manifolds with a stable framing (as is needed to equip the framed bordism groups with the structure of a ring) we use the second.

If we fix stable isomorphisms from each of
E,F,G,V,W
with
\mathbb{R}^{i}
for some
i
, these two constructions give the same homotopy class of stable framing up to the usual Koszul sign which comes from swapping factors of
\mathbb{R}
past each other.

4
A category of flow categories

4.1
Flow categories

Definition 46
.

A
flow category

\mathcal{F}
consists of a finite set
ob\mathcal{F}
(often abbreviated just to
\mathcal{F}
), a function (called the
grading
)
|\cdot|:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, and compact smooth manifolds with faces
\mathcal{F}_{xy}
of dimension
|x|-|y|-1
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{F}
. There are also
concatenation maps

c=c^{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{F}_{zy}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{xz}

which are the inclusion of the boundary face, such that the collection of these (allowing
z
to vary) forms a system of boundary faces for
\mathcal{F}_{xy}
. We further assume that the concatenation maps are associative, meaning the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{F}_{zw}\times\mathcal{F}_{wy}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{c}
