y_{0}=y,\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
,
z_{0}=z,\ldots,z_{k}
in
\mathcal{H}
,
u_{0},\ldots,u_{r}
in
\mathcal{I}
,
v_{0},\ldots,v_{s}
in
\mathcal{J}
and
w_{0},\ldots,w_{l}=w
in
\mathcal{K}
.

Note that in particular for an associator
\mathcal{Z}
, each
\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w}
has a system of boundary faces

\mathcal{F}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{x^{\prime}yz;w}

and

\mathcal{G}_{yy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{xy^{\prime}z;w}

and

\mathcal{H}_{zz^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{Z}_{xyz^{\prime};w}

and

\mathcal{W}_{xy;u}\times\mathcal{X}_{uz;w}

and

\mathcal{V}_{yz;v}\times\mathcal{Y}_{xv;w}

and

\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{K}_{w^{\prime}w}

for
x,x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}
,
y,y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
,
z,z^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{H}
,
u
in
\mathcal{I}
,
v
in
\mathcal{J}
and
w,w^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma 84
.

If there exists an associator
\mathcal{Z}
for
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
, then the two induced trilinear maps are equal:

\mathcal{X}_{*}\circ\mathcal{W}_{*}=\mathcal{Y}_{*}\circ\mathcal{V}_{*}:[*[i],\mathcal{F}]\otimes[*[j],\mathcal{G}]\otimes[*[k],\mathcal{H}]\rightarrow[*[i+j+k],\mathcal{K}]

Proof 5.4
.

\mathcal{B}\circ{\mathcal{Z}}
along with the proof of Lemma
58
provides a bordism between the bilinear maps
\mathcal{X}\circ(\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{Y}\circ(\mathcal{V}\circ\mathcal{B})
; the result then follows from Lemma
82
.

5.2
Bilinear maps: framings

We incorporate stable framings into Section
5.1
in the same way as in Section
4.3
. Similarly to Definition
66
, we have:

Definition 85
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
be framed flow categories. A
stable framing
on a bilinear map
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
consists of stable isomorphisms

st=st^{\mathcal{W}}_{xyz}:V_{x}\oplus V_{y}\oplus I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy;z}\to\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{z}

over each
\mathcal{W}_{xy,z}
with stabilising bundles
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{W}}_{xyz}
, along with, for each boundary face of each
\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}
, an embedding of the stabilising bundle of the induced framing on that face into
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{W}}_{xyz}
.

Here
I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy;z}\tau_{z}
is defined to be
T\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}\oplus\mathbb{R}\tau_{z}
(the same as in Definition
62
), with abstract gluing isomorphisms
\psi
defined similarly (noting that all boundary faces here are broken).

We require that these stable isomorphisms and embeddings, combined with those for
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{H}
and each lower-dimensional
\mathcal{W}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime};z^{\prime}}
, form systems of framings for each
\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}
.

{ex}

The analogues of Examples
5.1
and
5.1
hold for framed bilinear maps and framed morphisms.

Assume
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a framed bilinear map, and let
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
be framed morphisms. We equip each
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{W})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{W})
(constructed as in Section
5.1
) with framings, in exactly the same way we framed each
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}(\cdot,\cdot)
in Lemma
70
, and from this we frame
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{B}
in exactly the same way as in Lemma
71
. From this plus (the argument in) Lemma
72
, we have:

Lemma 86
.

The bilinear maps
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{H}
admit framings, and define well-defined framed morphisms up to framed bordism.

Similar to Lemma
80
(and by the same proof, with the usual incorporation of framings), we have:

Lemma 87
.

For all framed morphisms of flow categories
\mathcal{A}:*[i]\to\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{B}:*[j]\to\mathcal{G}
, we have that

[(\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{A})\circ\mathcal{B}]=[(\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{B})\circ\mathcal{A}]\in[*[i+j],\mathcal{H}]^{fr}

The same arguments as in Lemmas
72
and
80
prove

Lemma 88
.

This construction defines a well-defined bilinear map induced by
\mathcal{W}
:

\mathcal{W}_{*}:[*[i],\mathcal{F}]^{fr}\otimes[*[j],\mathcal{G}]^{fr}\to[*[i+j],\mathcal{H}]^{fr}

Next is bordisms of bilinear maps. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bordism from
\mathcal{W}
to
\mathcal{V}
, where
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
are bilinear maps, and assume
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
are all compatibly framed. By labelling the boundary faces of each
\mathcal{R}_{xy;z}
as broken or incoming/outgoing unbroken as in Definition
81
, from Section
4.2
we obtain abstract index bundles
I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy;z}
along with coherent abstract gluing isomorphisms over each boundary face.

Definition 89
.

A
framing
on the bordism
\mathcal{R}
consists of stable isomorphisms

st_{xy;z}:V_{x}\oplus V_{y}\oplus I^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy;z}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus V_{z}

over each
\mathcal{R}_{xy,z}
with stabilising bundle
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{R}}_{xy;z}
, along with, for each boundary face of
\mathcal{R}_{xy;z}
, an embedding of the stabilising bundle of the induced framing on that face into
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{R}}_{xyz}
.

We require that these stable isomorphisms and embeddings, combined with those of
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
and the framings for each lower-dimensional
\mathcal{R}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime};z^{\prime}}
, form systems of framings for each
\mathcal{R}_{xy;z}
.

Incorporating framings into the proof of Lemma
82
just as in Lemma
71
, we have that:

Lemma 90
.

Let
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be framed bilinear maps,
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}
a framed morphism and
\mathcal{R}
a framed bordism from
\mathcal{W}
to
\mathcal{V}
. Then
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{V}\circ\mathcal{A}
are framed bordant bilinear maps
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
.

In particular,
\mathcal{W}_{*}=\mathcal{V}_{*}
.

We now move onto associators. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be an associator for the decuplet
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
. Note all boundary faces of each
\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w}
are broken.

Definition 91
.

A
framing
on the associator
\mathcal{Z}
consists of stable isomorphisms

st_{xyz;w}:V_{x}\oplus V_{y}\oplus V_{z}\oplus I^{\mathcal{Z}}_{xyz;w}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus V_{w}

over each
\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w}
with stabilising bundle
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{Z}}_{xyz;w}
, along with, for each boundary face of
\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w}
, an embedding of the stabilising bundle of the induced framing on that face into
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{Z}}_{xyz;w}
.

We require that these, combined with the framings for all items in the decuplet and the framings for each lower-dimensional
\mathcal{Z}_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime};w^{\prime}}
, form systems of framings for each
\mathcal{Z}_{xyz;w}
.

Similarly to Lemma
84
, by incorporating framings in the usual way, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 92
.

If there exists an framed associator
\mathcal{Z}
for a framed decuplet
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
as above, then the two induced trilinear maps are equal:

\mathcal{X}_{*}\circ\mathcal{W}_{*}=\mathcal{Y}_{*}\circ\mathcal{V}_{*}:[*[i],\mathcal{F}]^{fr}\otimes[*[j],\mathcal{G}]^{fr}\otimes[*[k],\mathcal{H}]^{fr}\to[*[i+j+k],\mathcal{K}]^{fr}

5.3
Morse complex of a flow category

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a flow category (at this point not necessarily framed). We define

CM_{i}(\mathcal{M})=\oplus_{|x|=i}\mathbb{Z}/2\langle x\rangle.

We define a differential
\partial:CM_{i}(\mathcal{M})\to CM_{i-1}(\mathcal{M})
by setting

\partial(x)=\sum_{y:|y|=|x|-1}|\mathcal{M}_{xy}|\cdot y

where
\mathcal{M}_{xy}
is a manifold of dimension
|x|-|y|-1=0
and
|\mathcal{M}_{xy}|\in\mathbb{Z}/2
denotes its cardinality.

Lemma 93
.

\partial
is a differential.

The proof is a simplification of the case of the Morse complex of a framed flow category given below in Lemma
95
.

There is a canonical isomorphism of rings
\Gamma:\Omega^{fr}_{0}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{Z}
between the 0-dimensional framed bordism group and
\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 94
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a framed flow category.
For each object
x
, there are two homotopy classes of stable isomorphism
V_{x}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{-|x|}
. We let
F(x)
be the set of such homotopy classes of isomorphisms.

We define the
Morse chain complex

CM_{*}(\mathcal{M})
to be the chain complex with underlying graded abelian groups given by

CM_{i}(\mathcal{M}):=\left(\bigoplus_{|x|=i,\phi\in F(x)}\mathbb{Z}\phi\right)/A_{i}

where
A_{i}
is the subgroup generated by expressions

\sum_{\phi\in F(x)}\phi

for all
x
. This has differential
\partial
, which is defined as follows. We first note that each
\mathcal{M}_{xy}
is not naturally a stably framed manifold, but does acquire a natural stable framing if we fix stable isomorphisms between
V_{x}
and
V_{y}
and
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for appropriate
N
.

Let
x
be an object in
\mathcal{M}
, with index
i=\mu(x)
. For a generator
\phi
in
F(x)
,

\partial\phi:=\sum_{\mu(y)=i-1}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{xy})\psi_{y}

where we choose some
\psi_{y}
in
F(y)
for each
y
, and
\mathcal{M}_{xy}
is equipped with the stable framing it acquires from
\phi
and
\psi_{y}
. Then
\partial
is a linear map which sends
A_{i}
to
A_{i-1}
and so descends to a well-defined linear map
CM_{i}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow CM_{i-1}(\mathcal{M})
, independent of the choices of
\psi_{y}
.

{rmk}

Note that an evidently equivalent definition to this is to initially pick a (stable) isomorphism
\phi_{x}:V_{x}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{N_{x}+\mu(x)}
for each
x
, and only take one generator for each
x
, and then to not quotient by the subgroups
A_{*}
, observing that the resulting complex is independent of the choices of isomorphisms
\phi_{x}
. This is in some sense a nicer definition but requires us to keep track of more choices along the way.

Lemma 95
.

\partial
is a differential. Equivalently,

\partial\circ\partial=0.

Proof 5.5
.

Let
x
be an object of
\mathcal{M}
, of index
i=\mu(x)
. Choose
\phi
in
F(x)
. Then

\partial\circ\partial(x)=\sum\limits_{\mu(y)=i-1}\sum\limits_{\mu(z)=i-2}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{xy})\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{yz})\,\theta_{z}

where we choose
\psi_{y}
in
F(y)
and
\theta_{z}
in
F(z)
for each
y
,
z
, which induces framings on
\mathcal{M}_{xy}
and
\mathcal{M}_{yz}
. We will show that the coefficient of each
\theta_{z}
is 0, which implies the whole sum is 0.

The coefficient of each
\theta_{z}
is

\sum\limits_{\mu(y)=i-1}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{xy})\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{yz})

which, since
\Gamma
is a ring map, is equal to

\Gamma\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\mu(y)=i-1}\mathcal{M}_{xy}\times\mathcal{M}_{yz}\right)

which is equal to

\Gamma\left(\partial\mathcal{M}_{xz}\right)

and hence vanishes.

Definition 96
.

The
Morse homology

HM_{*}(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{M}
is defined to be the homology of the chain complex
CM_{*}(\mathcal{M})
.

Definition 97
.

Given a morphism
\mathcal{W}
of framed flow categories from
\mathcal{M}^{0}
to
\mathcal{M}^{1}
, there is an induced map

CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right):CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}^{0}\right)\rightarrow CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}^{1}\right)

defined as follows. Let
x
be an object in
\mathcal{M}^{0}
, with index
i=|x|
. For a generator
\phi
in
F(x)
,

CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\phi:=\sum\limits_{|y|=i}\Gamma\left(\mathcal{W}_{xy}\right)\,\psi_{y}

where we choose some
\psi_{y}
in
F(y)
for each
y
, and
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
is equipped with the stable framing it acquires from
\phi
and
\psi
. Then
CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)
is a linear map which sends subgroups
A_{i}^{0}
to
A_{i}^{1}
and so descends to a well-defined linear map
CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}^{0}\right)
to
CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{M}^{1}\right)
, independent of the choices of
\psi_{y}
.

Lemma 98
.

CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)
is a chain map. Equivalently,

\partial^{1}\circ CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)=CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\circ\partial^{0}.

Proof 5.6
.

Let
x
be an object of
\mathcal{M}^{0}
, of index
i=|x|
. Choose
\phi
in
F(x)
. Then

\partial^{1}\circ CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)(x)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{1}\\
|y|=i\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathcal{M}^{1}\\
|z|=i-1\end{subarray}}\Gamma\left(\mathcal{W}_{xy}\right)\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}^{1}_{yz}(y,z)\right)\theta_{z}

where we choose
\psi_{y}
in
F(y)
and
\theta_{z}
in
F(z)
for each
y,z
, similarly to the proof of Lemma
95
. Similarly,

CM_{*}\left(\mathcal{W}\right)\circ\partial^{0}(x)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{0}\\
|y|=i-1\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathcal{M}^{1}\\
|z|=i-1\end{subarray}}\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}^{0}_{xy}\right)\Gamma\left(\mathcal{W}_{yz}\right)\theta_{z}.

The coefficient of each
\theta_{z}
in the difference between these expressions is

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{1}\\
|y|=i\end{subarray}}\Gamma\left(\mathcal{W}_{xy}\right)\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}^{1}_{yz}\right)-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{0}\\
|y|=i-1\end{subarray}}\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}^{0}_{xy}\right)\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}^{01}_{yz}\right)

which, since
\Gamma
is a ring map, is

\Gamma\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{1}\\
|y|=i\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times\mathcal{M}^{1}_{yz}\sqcup\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{M}^{0}\\
|y|=i-1\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{M}^{0}_{xy}\times\mathcal{W}_{yz}}\right)

which is equal to

\Gamma\left(\partial\mathcal{W}_{xy}\right)

and hence vanishes. Note that
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{0}_{xy}\times\mathcal{W}_{yz}}
appears with the framing reversed; see Remark
3.6
.

Now let
\mathcal{M}^{i}
be framed flow categories, for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{M}^{0}\times\mathcal{M}^{1}\to\mathcal{M}^{2}
be a framed bilinear map.

Lemma 99
.

The framed bilinear map
\mathcal{R}
induces a degree zero product

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}:CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{0})\otimes CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{1})\longrightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{2})

which descends to homology.
If
\mathcal{R}
admits a framed associator, then the product on homology is associative.

Proof 5.7
.

Fix objects
x
and
y
of
\mathcal{M}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{1}
of degrees
|x|=i
and
|y|=j
. For each
z\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
with
|z|=i+j
we have a zero-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}
and we set

\mu_{\mathcal{R}}(\phi_{x}\otimes\phi_{y})=\sum_{|z|=i+j}\Gamma(\mathcal{R}_{xy;z})\,\phi_{z}

where
\phi_{\bullet}\in F(\bullet)
for
\bullet=x,y,z
and the choices of
\phi_{\bullet}
equip
\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}
with a stable framing. As in the construction of the differential on
CM_{i}(\mathcal{M})
above, the map does not depend on the choices of the
\phi_{\bullet}
. The fact that
\mathcal{W}_{xy;z}
, with
|z|=|x|+|y|-1
, has a system of boundary faces indexed by

\mathcal{M}^{0}_{xx^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{R}_{x^{\prime}y;z},\,\mathcal{M}^{1}_{yy^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{R}_{xy^{\prime};z},\,\mathcal{R}_{xy;z^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{z^{\prime}z}

for appropriate
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}

shows that
\mu_{\mathcal{R}}
satisfies the Leibniz rule and hence descends to a map on homology. The proof of the final claim is analogous.

5.4
Truncations

Definition 100
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories. A
pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism

\mathcal{W}
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
consists of compact smooth manifolds with faces
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
(of dimension
|x|-|y|
) for all
x
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
|x|-|y|\leq n
, along with maps

c=c^{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{W}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{xy}

and

c=c^{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}_{xz}\times\mathcal{G}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{xy}

which are compatible with
c^{\mathcal{F}}
and
c^{\mathcal{G}}
, which define a system of faces

\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
x_{0}=x,\ldots x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
.

A
pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordism

\mathcal{R}
between two pre-
\tau_{\leq n-1}
-morphisms
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
consists of compact smooth manifolds with faces
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
(of dimension
|x|-|y|
+ 1), for all
x
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
|x|-|y|+1\leq n
, along with maps

c=c^{\mathcal{R}}:\mathcal{W}_{xy},\mathcal{V}_{xy}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{xy}

and

c=c^{\mathcal{R}}:\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{R}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{xy}

and

c=c^{\mathcal{R}}:\mathcal{R}_{xz}\times\mathcal{G}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{xy}

compatible with
c^{\mathcal{F}}
,
c^{\mathcal{G}}
,
c^{\mathcal{W}}
,
c^{\mathcal{V}}
, such that the images of the faces in the domains defines a system of faces for
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
(after deleting repeats). More precisely, there is a system of faces for
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
given by the following:

\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

and

\mathcal{V}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

and

\mathcal{R}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
x_{0}=x,\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

{rmk}

A more intuitive description is the following. A pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}
is like a morphism of flow categories
\mathcal{W}
except we only require the manifolds with faces
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
to exist when their dimension is
\leq n
, but when they do exist they have the same concatenation maps, defining a system of faces in the same way as before. A similar description applies to pre-bordisms.

{rmk}

Similarly to with morphisms and bordisms, we equip any pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism or pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordism we encounter with a system of collars, compatible with those on
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
(and
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
in the case of a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordism).

{ex}

Any morphism of flow categories
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
determines a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
by forgetting the manifolds
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
whenever
|x|-|y|>n
.

Similarly, whenever
m\geq n
, any pre-
\tau_{\leq m}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}
determines a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}
in the same way.

Clearly composing these truncation operations is associative, i.e.
\tau_{\leq n}\tau_{\leq m}\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}
, for
n\leq m
.

Note that these operations are not necessarily surjective: not every pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
is
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}
for
\mathcal{W}
some pre-
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism.

There are similar truncation operations for pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordisms.

Definition 101
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories. A
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism

\mathcal{W}
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
is a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
for some pre-
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
. We call
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
an
extension
of
\mathcal{F}
.

A
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordism
between two
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
is a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordism
\mathcal{R}
between
\tau_{\leq n-1}\mathcal{W}
and
\tau_{\leq n-1}\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathcal{R}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
some pre-
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-bordism between
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
. We call
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
an
extension
of
\mathcal{R}
.

We emphasise that in these definitions, the extensions
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are assumed to exist, but are not kept track of as part of the data.

{rmk}

Similarly to Remark
5.4
, we think of these as morphisms (or bordisms of morphisms) of flow categories, where we only specify the manifolds with faces
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
when their dimension is
\leq n
(again with the appropriate system of faces), and such that there exist manifolds with faces
\mathcal{W}_{xy}
when the dimension is
n+1
with the appropriate system of faces, but without keeping track of these
(n+1)
-dimensional manifolds with faces as part of the data.

{rmk}

We may think of a morphism of flow categories as a
\tau_{\leq\infty}
-morphism, or equivalently as a pre-
\tau_{\leq\infty}
-morphism.

{ex}

Similarly to Example
5.4
, any morphism of flow categories
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
determines a
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{W}
by forgetting all manifolds of dimension
\geq n
.

{ex}

A pre-
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphism
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
determines a linear map
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2)\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, defined by

CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})(x):=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{G}\,,\,|y|=|x|}|\mathcal{W}_{xy}|\cdot y

for
x
in
\mathcal{F}
. Moreover,

\mathcal{W}
is a
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphism if and only if
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})
is a chain map.

If
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq 0}\mathcal{V}
for some morphism
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
, then this is the same as the construction in Section
5.3
.

{ex}

Given a morphism
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, there is a natural isomorphism

CM_{*}(\Cone(\mathcal{W}))\simeq\Cone(CM_{*}(\mathcal{W}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{F})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{G}))

{rmk}

Example
5.4
is one of the main reasons we will define a category built out of
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms, rather that pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms.

Similarly to Definition
51
, we give the following definition.

Definition 102
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories. Define
[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq n}}
to be the set of
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, modulo the equivalence relation generated by

1.

\mathcal{W}\sim\mathcal{V}
if
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordant.

2.

\mathcal{W}\sim\mathcal{V}
if
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are diffeomorphic.

This is an abelian group under disjoint union, with unit given by the empty morphism.

{ex}

Truncation as in Example
5.4
determines morphisms of abelian groups

[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]\xrightarrow{\tau_{\leq m}}[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq m}}\xrightarrow{\tau_{\leq n}}[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq n}}

for
m\geq n
.

Lemma 103
.

There is a natural isomorphism
\Xi:[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq 0}}\rightarrow\Hom(CM_{*}(\mathcal{F}),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G}))
(where the target is the set of chain maps
CM_{*}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G})
up to homotopy).

Proof 5.8
.

We first show that
[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq 0}}\to\Hom(CM_{*}(\mathcal{F}),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G}))
is injective. Suppose
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are flow
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphisms which are bordant, via a bordism
\mathcal{R}
. Recall that the bordism relation means that we have compact smooth manifolds with faces
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
of dimensions
|x|-|y|+1
for
x\in\mathcal{F}
and
y\in\mathcal{G}
(with appropriate faces). In particular we can define a linear map
CM_{*}(\mathcal{F})\to CM_{*+1}(\mathcal{G})
via

x\mapsto\sum_{|y|=|x|+1}\mathcal{R}_{xy}\,y

The bordism relation implies this defines a chain homotopy between the chain maps
x\mapsto\sum_{|y|=|x|}\mathcal{W}_{xy}\,y
and
x\mapsto\sum_{|y|=|x|}\mathcal{V}_{xy}\,y
. A corresponding simpler argument when
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are related by diffeomorphism then shows injectivity of
\Xi
. Analogously, any linear map
CM_{*}(\mathcal{F})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{G})
, say
x\mapsto\sum_{|y|=|x|}n_{xy}y
for integers
n_{xy}
, can be viewed as a collection of framed
0
-manifolds
n_{xy}
which define a pre-
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphism. The condition that the linear map is a chain map exactly says this lifts to a
\tau_{\leq 0}
-morphism, which gives surjectivity of
\Xi
.

Given pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, we can form their composition,
\mathcal{V}\circ\mathcal{W}
, identically to the case of morphisms of flow categories.

We first define
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{xy}=\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{xy}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{V})
as in Equation
5
whenever
|x|-|y|\leq n
. The
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{xy}
are manifolds with faces by the same argument as before, with a system of faces given by the same description. Choosing compatible framed functions
(f_{xy},s_{xy})
as before and defining
\mathcal{U}_{xy}=f_{xy}^{-1}\{p\}
whenever
|x|-|y|\leq n
. defines a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
morphism
\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H}
, which we define
\mathcal{V}\circ\mathcal{G}
to be.
Composition of this form is compatible with further truncation: if
n>k
and we apply the above construction to
\tau_{\leq k}\mathcal{W}
and
\tau_{\leq k}\mathcal{V}
and take the restrictions of the same framed functions functions, the output will be exactly
\tau_{\leq k}\mathcal{U}
.

Definition 104
.

If
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
are
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms, we choose extensions
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
and compose these as above to get a pre-
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Then we define the
composition
of
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
to be the
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

{rmk}

As a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism,
\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is the composition of
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
. However if we composed
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}
as pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms and used different choices of framed functions, we may produce a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism that does not admit an extension, which is why we instead take the truncation of the composition of the extensions
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 105
.

Composition of
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms is compatible with the equivalence relation defined in Definition
101
, and induces a well-defined bilinear map

\circ:[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}]_{\tau_{\leq n}}\otimes[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau_{\leq n}}\rightarrow[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H}]_{\tau_{\leq n}}.

Proof 5.9
.

Identical to that of Lemma
56
but only considering manifolds of appropriate dimensions.

Lemma 106
.

Composition of
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms is associative.

Proof 5.10
.

Identical to that of Lemma
57
but only considering manifolds of appropriate dimensions.

Similarly we may define
\tau_{\leq n}
bilinear maps, bordisms of bilinear maps and associators, satisfying the same properties as we saw before; in particular they can be associatively composed.

We have thus shown that there is a category,
\Flow_{\tau_{\leq n}}
, with objects flow categories, and morphisms
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\tau_{\leq n}}
. Compatibility of truncations with composition defines functors

\Flow\rightarrow\Flow_{\tau_{\leq m}}\rightarrow\Flow_{\tau_{\leq n}}

(10)

whenever
m\geq n
which are suitably associative.

Generalising Lemma
103
, we conjecture the following.

Conjecture 107
.

The category
\Flow_{\tau_{\leq n}}
is equivalent to the category with objects perfect
MO
-modules, and morphisms
M\rightarrow N
given by homotopy classes of
\tau_{\leq n}MO
-linear maps
M\otimes_{MO}\tau_{\leq n}MO\rightarrow N\otimes_{MO}\tau_{\leq n}MO
, where
\tau_{\leq n}
denotes the truncation of a spectrum with respect to the standard
t
-structure.

Furthermore this equivalence should be compatible (in an appropriate sense) with the equivalence of Conjecture
60
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are framed flow categories, with framing stable vector spaces
V_{x}
and
V_{y}
for
x\in\mathcal{F}
and
y\in\mathcal{G}
. A pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-framed morphism is a pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
morphism for which the stable isomorphisms
st_{xy}
from Definition
66
are defined and satisfy the same compatibility whenever they can i.e. whenever
|x|-|y|\leq n
. This is a
\tau_{\leq n}
-framed morphism if it arises by truncation from a pre-
\tau_{\leq(n+1)}
-framed morphism as usual. All of the definitions and properties of
\Flow^{fr}
are identical in this truncated setting, except that the various diagrams now only make sense when the degrees of the objects are constrained. In particular, we have the analogue

{\Flow}^{fr}\rightarrow{\Flow}^{fr}_{\tau_{\leq m}}\rightarrow{\Flow}^{fr}_{\tau_{\leq n}}

(11)

for functors of truncations of framed flow categories, again associative. Entirely analogously to Conjecture
107
, we have:

Conjecture 108
.

The category
\Flow^{fr}_{\tau_{\leq n}}
is equivalent to the category with objects perfect
\mathbb{S}
-modules, and morphisms
M\rightarrow N
given by homotopy classes of
\tau_{\leq n}\mathbb{S}
-linear maps
M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}\tau_{\leq n}\mathbb{S}\rightarrow N\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}\tau_{\leq n}\mathbb{S}
.

5.5
Obstruction theory

In this section, we perform some more involved homological algebra in the category of flow categories. In each case, we provide the analogous result from (ordinary) homological algebra as motivation; we expect that these should be equivalent under Conjectures
107
and
108
.

Theorem 109
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories such that either
\mathcal{F}=*[i]
or
\mathcal{G}=*[i]
for some
i
. Let
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
be a
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism. Then there is a class

[\mathcal{O}]=[\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W})]\in\Hom\left(CM_{*+n+2}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};\Omega_{n+1})\right)

such that
[\mathcal{O}]
vanishes if and only if
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{V}
, for
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
some
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism.

Similarly if
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{W}
are all framed, there is a class

[\mathcal{O}^{fr}]=[\mathcal{O}^{fr}(\mathcal{W})]\in\Hom\left(CM_{*+n+2}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};\Omega_{n+1}^{fr})\right)

such that
[\mathcal{O}^{fr}]
vanishes if and only if
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{V}
, for
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
some framed
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism.

We conjecture that the condition that either
\mathcal{F}
or
\mathcal{G}
is
*[i]
is unnecessary.

We first prove the unframed case, then incorporate framings into the same proof.

Proof 5.11

(Proof without framings)
.

We assume that
\mathcal{F}=*[i]
here, the case where
\mathcal{G}=*[i]
is similar.

Choose
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
an extension of
\mathcal{W}
. We first construct a linear map

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):CM_{*+n+2}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2)\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\Omega_{n+1})

We define

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}=\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):=\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{w\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*w}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)\right)/\sim

for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+2
, where
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined so that as a topological space,
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
is the coequaliser of the diagram

\bigsqcup\limits_{w,v\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*w}\times\mathcal{G}_{wv}\times\mathcal{G}_{vy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}_{u,v}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup\limits_{w\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*w}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)_{s}

where the two maps send
(a,b,c,u,v)
to
(a,\mathcal{C}(b,c,u),v)
and
(\mathcal{C}(a,b,v),c,u)
respectively.

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
, and in particular a system of boundary faces given by
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*w}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times\{0\}
for
w\in\mathcal{G}
. Note that all these faces are compact.

We choose framed functions
(f_{*y},s_{*y})
on each
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
, noting that on all proper faces each
f_{*y}
is negative.

Then define

\mathcal{Z}_{*y}=\mathcal{Z}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):=f_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}

This is a closed smooth manifold of dimension
n+1
, and its bordism class
[\mathcal{Z}_{*y}]
in the bordism group
\Omega_{n+1}
is independent of the choice of
f_{*y}
, though it does depend on the choice of extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
(we will use this dependence later).

We define the linear map
\mathcal{O}
by

\mathcal{O}(*):=\sum\limits_{i-|y|=n+2}[\mathcal{Z}_{*y}]\cdot y

We next show this is a chain map. This is equivalent to showing that the closed manifold

\bigsqcup\limits_{s\in\mathcal{G},|s|-|y|=1}\mathcal{Z}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}

represents the zero class in the bordism group
\Omega_{n+1}
(i.e. it bounds a compact smooth manifold with boundary), for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+3
. We note here that since a closed smooth manifold which bounds a topological manifold also bounds a smooth manifold, it would be sufficient (and easier) to show that this class is zero in the topological bordism group, but this would not adapt so well to the framed setting.

We define manifolds with faces

\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}:=\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{w\in\mathcal{G},|w|-|y|>1}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{xw}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)\right)/\sim

for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+3
, where
\sim
is defined so that as a topological space,
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}
is the coequaliser of the diagram

\bigsqcup_{v,w\in\mathcal{G},|w|-|y|>1}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*v}\times\mathcal{G}_{vw}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}_{u,v}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup_{w\in\mathcal{G},|w|-|y|>1}\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{xw}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)

where the two maps send
(a,b,c,u,v)
to
(a,\mathcal{C}(b,c,u),v)
and
(\mathcal{C}(a,b,v),c,u)
respectively. Note that there are natural embeddings

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}

for all
s
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
|s|-|y|=1
, and that these have disjoint images.

\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}
has a system of faces given by

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
j>1
or
|y_{j-1}|-|y_{j}|>1
, along with the codimension 1 faces

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}

for
s
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
|s|-|y|=1
. Note that the only non-compact faces are the ones of the form
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}
.

Now choose framed functions
(g_{*y},t_{*y})
on each
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}
, compatible with the
(f_{*s},s_{*s})
on each
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{*y}=g_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{*y}
is a compact smooth manifold with boundary

\bigsqcup\limits_{s\in\mathcal{G},|s|-|y|=1}\mathcal{Z}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}

which implies that
\mathcal{O}
is a chain map.

We now assume that
\mathcal{O}
is a null-homotopic chain map, i.e. its class in

\Hom\left(CM_{*+n+2}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2),CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\Omega_{n+1})\right)

is 0. We show that there is some other choice of extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime})=0
.

This assumption says that there is some linear map

\mathcal{P}:CM_{*+n+1}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2)\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\Omega_{n+1})

such that
\mathcal{O}(*)=d\mathcal{P}(*)
. More explicitly,
\mathcal{P}
sends
*
to

\sum\limits_{y\in\mathcal{G},i-|y|=n+1}[\mathcal{P}_{*y}]\cdot y

for some closed manifolds
\mathcal{P}_{*y}
, where
[\mathcal{P}_{*y}]
represents the bordism classes in the bordism group
\Omega_{n+1}
of each
\mathcal{P}_{*y}
, and such that

\bigsqcup\limits_{s\in\mathcal{G},|s|-|y|=1}\mathcal{P}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}

is cobordant to
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}
for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+2
.

We define
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}_{*y}:=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*y}
for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|\leq n
, and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}_{*y}:=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*y}\sqcup\mathcal{P}_{*y}
for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+1
. Then we see that

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime})=\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})\bigsqcup\limits_{s\in\mathcal{G},|s|-|y|=1}\mathcal{P}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}\times[0,\varepsilon)

so for appropriate choices of framed functions
(f_{*x},s_{*x})
, we see that

\mathcal{Z}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{Z}_{*y}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})\bigsqcup\limits_{s\in\mathcal{G},|s|-|y|=1}\mathcal{P}_{*s}\times\mathcal{G}_{sy}

Each of these manifolds is null-cobordant, so
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime})=0
.

Replacing
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}
, we may now assume that
\mathcal{O}=0
. We build a pre-
\tau_{\leq n+2}
-morphism
\mathcal{U}
such that
\tau_{\leq n+1}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, which implies that
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
is the desired
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism.

We define
\mathcal{U}_{*y}=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*y}
for all
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|\leq n+1
. If we were to set
\mathcal{U}_{*y}
to be
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
for
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
i-|y|=n+2
, it has the right system of faces for
\mathcal{U}
to be a pre-
\tau_{\leq n+2}
-morphism, but is non-compact. Instead consider the map
f_{*y}:\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
f_{*y}^{-1}(-\infty,1]
is compact, and by assumption
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}=f_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
is a closed smooth manifold which is nullcobordant, so we can glue such a nullcobordism onto
f_{*y}^{-1}(-\infty,1]
, and set
\mathcal{U}_{*y}
to be this new manifold. Then by construction
\mathcal{U}
is a pre-
\tau_{\leq n+2}
-morphism such that
\tau_{n+1}\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
.

We now assume that
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{V}
for
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
some
\tau_{\leq n+1}
-morphism. Choose an extension
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{V}
. Then when
*-|y|\leq n+2
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{*y}
is a compact manifold with faces with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
. When
*-|y|=n+2
, the collar neighbourhood theorem for manifolds with faces implies that there is a neigbourhood of all the faces in
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{*y}
diffeomorphic to
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
. Choosing
f_{*y}
as in the definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}
,

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{*y}\setminus f_{*y}^{-1}(-\infty,1)

is a compact manifold with boundary
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}
. Therefore the linear map
\mathcal{O}
is 0.

Proof 5.12

(Incorporating framings)
.

We incorporate framings into the unframed proof, assuming
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
are all framed.

We first note that all boundary faces of each
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
are broken. We frame each of these manifolds compatibly.

Inductively in
i-|*|
, we choose vector bundles
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}_{*y}
over each
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{*y}
, along with, for each boundary face of
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{*y}
, an embedding of the stabilising bundle over that face into
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}_{*y}
, which agree on the image of the embeddings from the codimension two faces.

For each
y\in\mathcal{G}
, we define the stable isomorphism

st_{*y}:V_{*[i]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}_{*y}\to\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{y}

to be given by, on each
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*w}\times\mathcal{G}_{wy}\times[0,\varepsilon)
, the composition

V_{*[i]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{R}}}_{*y}\xrightarrow{\psi^{-1}}V_{*[i]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}_{*w}\oplus I^{\mathcal{G}}_{wy}\xrightarrow{st^{\mathcal{G}}\circ st^{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{y}

extended along the embedding
\iota
into
\mathbb{T}^{\mathcal{R}}_{xz}
.

These glue on overlaps just as in Lemma
70
, and similarly are compatible on boundary faces in the sense that they (combined with the other framings) form systems of framings on each
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
.

As in Lemma
70
, we use the sections
s_{*y}
to then obtain stable framings

st^{\mathcal{Z}}:V_{*[i]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{Z}}_{*y}\to V_{y}

on each closed manifold
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}
. We choose stable isomorphisms
V_{q}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{|q|}
for each
q\in\mathcal{G}
or
q=*[i]
, then this framing on each
\mathcal{Z}_{*y}
determines a class
[\mathcal{Z}_{*y}]
in
\Omega^{fr}_{*}
.

We then define
\mathcal{O}^{fr}
in the same way as the unframed case, but now using the fact that the manifolds in question are framed, so
\mathcal{O}^{fr}
is now a linear map

\mathcal{O}^{fr}=\mathcal{O}^{fr}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):CM_{*+n+2}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\Omega^{fr}_{n+1})

Note all boundary faces of each
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{*y}
are broken. We frame them in exactly the same way as the
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
, to give stable framings

st^{\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}}_{*y}:V_{*[i]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}}_{*y}\to\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{y}

which are compatible with the framings on the boundary faces in the sense that they form systems of faces over each
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*s}
; these framings induce framings on each
\mathcal{L}_{*y}
, which implies that
\mathcal{O}^{fr}
is a chain map.

The assumption says that there is some linear map

\mathcal{P}^{fr}:CM_{*+n+1}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\Omega^{fr}_{n+1})

such that
\mathcal{O}^{fr}(*)=d\mathcal{P}^{fr}(*)
. By replacing
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
with a different extension
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{W}
by attaching disjoint copies of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{*y}
to each
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{*y}
when
i-|y|=n+1
as in the unframed case, we may assume that
\mathcal{O}^{fr}=0
.

We take the same (unframed) pre-
\tau_{\leq n+2}
morphism
\mathcal{U}
as in the unframed case, and by using the framings on the
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{*y}
and the framing of the nullbordism of each
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{*y}
, and gluing these framings together on the overlap, we obtain framings on
\mathcal{U}
.

The claim that if
\mathcal{W}=\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{V}
for some
\tau_{\leq n+1}
morphism
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
then the associated linear map
\mathcal{O}^{fr}
is trivial is essentially the same as in the unframed case.

Theorem
109
should be considered an analogue in
\Flow
(respectively
\Flow^{fr}
) of (a special case of) the following fact in homological algebra, applied to the ring spectra
MO
(respectively
\mathbb{S}
).

Proposition 110
.

Let
R
be a
(-1)
-connected dga or ring spectrum, and let
M
and
N
be objects in
R\modules
, the category of dg modules or module spectra over
R
. All tensor products we take are assumed to be derived. Let

[\phi]\in\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R\right)

be a morphism in the derived category of
\tau_{\leq n}R
(here
\tau
refers to truncation with respect to the standard
t
-structure), with some representative
\phi
in
[\phi]
.

Then there is a class

[\mathcal{O}]\in\Hom_{HR_{0}\modules}\left(\Sigma^{-n-2}M\otimes_{R}HR_{0},N\otimes_{R}HR_{n+1}\right)

(here
HR_{i}
is the appropriate Eilenberg-Maclane chain complex or spectrum) which vanishes if and only if
[\phi]
is in the image of the natural map

\tau_{\leq n}:\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n+1}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R\right)\rightarrow\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R\right)

{rmk}

Formulating this at the level of
HR_{0}
-modules is analogous to working with chain complexes in Theorem
109
.

Proof 5.13

(Sketch proof)
.

There is a fibration sequence in
\tau_{\leq n+1}R\modules
(using the fact that
R
is
(-1)
-connected here):

N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R\rightarrow N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R\rightarrow N\otimes_{R}\Sigma^{n+2}HR_{n+1}

and therefore an exact sequence

\textstyle{\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n+1}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n+1}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n+1}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n+1}R,N\otimes_{R}\Sigma^{n+2}HR_{n+1}\right)}

We identify the middle term with

\Hom_{\tau_{\leq n}R\modules}\left(M\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R,N\otimes_{R}\tau_{\leq n}R\right)

the final term with

\Hom_{HR_{0}\modules}\left(\Sigma^{-n-2}M\otimes_{R}HR_{0},N\otimes_{R}HR_{n+1}\right)

and take
[\mathcal{O}]
to be
f([\phi])
.

Theorem 111
.

Let
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
be a morphism of flow categories such that the induced map
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W};\mathbb{Z}/2):CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{G};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a quasiisomorphism over
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Then the induced map
\mathcal{W}_{*}:\left[*[u],\mathcal{F}\right]\to\left[*[u],\mathcal{G}\right]
is surjective for all
u
.

Similarly if
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{W}
are all framed and
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W};\mathbb{Z}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{F};\mathbb{Z})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{G},\mathbb{Z})
is a quasiisomorphism over
\mathbb{Z}
, then the induced map
\mathcal{W}_{*}:[*[u],\mathcal{F}]^{fr}\to[*[u],\mathcal{G}]^{fr}
is surjective for all
u
.

We conjecture (but do not require for our purposes) that the same statement holds for injectivity as well as surjectivity.

Proof 5.14

(Proof without framings)
.

We show that
\mathcal{W}_{*}:\left[*[u],\mathcal{F}\right]_{\tau_{\leq v}}\to\left[*[u],\mathcal{G}\right]_{\tau_{\leq v}}
is surjective for each
v
inductively; this holds for
v=0
by assumption. Assume this holds for
v
; we will show it holds for
v+1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}:*[u]\to\mathcal{G}
be a
\tau_{\leq v+1}
-morphism. By assumption,
\tau_{\leq v}\mathcal{B}
lifts to a
\tau_{\leq v}
-morphism
\mathcal{A}:*[u]\to\mathcal{F}
, and there is a bordism
\mathcal{R}
from
\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{A}
to
\tau_{\leq v}\mathcal{B}
.

Much more explicitly, we first choose an extension
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{B}
, and assume that there are compact manifolds with faces
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}
for each
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
u-|x|\leq v+1
of dimension
u-|x|
, each of which has a system of faces given by

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}=x
in
\mathcal{F}
(and in particular a system of boundary faces given by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x^{\prime}x}
for
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}
), as well as compact manifolds with faces
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
for each
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|\leq v
of dimension
u-|y|+1
, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{B}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
, and

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
. In particular,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
has a system of boundary faces given by
\mathcal{B}_{*y}
,
\mathcal{A}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}
for
x
in
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y^{\prime}y}
for
y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

We next define an element
(a,r)
in

CM_{u-v-2}\left(\mathcal{F};\Omega_{v+1}\right)\oplus CM_{u-v-1}\left(\mathcal{G};\Omega_{v+1}\right)

For
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
u-|x|=v+2
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{*x}
to be the coequaliser of the following.

\bigsqcup\limits_{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x^{\prime\prime}x^{\prime}x}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup\limits_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x^{\prime}x}\times[0,\varepsilon)

By Lemma
38
, this is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\{0\}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}=x
in
\mathcal{F}
. In particular, all these faces are compact.

Choose framed functions
(f_{*x},s_{*x})
on each
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{*x}
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{*x}:=f_{*x}^{-1}\{1\}
. Now define
a
by

a:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{F}\\
u-|x|=v+2\end{subarray}}[\mathcal{S}_{*x}]\cdot x\in CM_{u-v-2}\left(\mathcal{F};\Omega_{v+1}\right)

Note that
a
is a specific instance of the obstruction chain (written there as
\mathcal{O}
) constructed in (the proof of) Theorem
109
, and therefore has already been shown to be a closed chain.

For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+1
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
to be the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{B}_{*y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)\times[0,-\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}_{*y}\times[0,-\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{F}\\
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{x;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}_{*;x^{\prime}x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Note the copies of
[0,-\varepsilon)
in some places instead of
[0,\varepsilon)
; this will be relevant when we work in the framed setting. Here the two maps out of each
\mathcal{B}_{*y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)\times[0,-\varepsilon)
send
(a,b,u,v)
to
\alpha(a,b,u,v):=(\mathcal{C}(a,b;u),v)
and
\beta(a,b,u,v):=(\mathcal{C}(a;-v),b,u)
respectively.

Each
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
is a manifold with corners by Lemma
37
(noting
[0,-\varepsilon)
is naturally diffeomorphic to
[0,\varepsilon)
), and by applying Lemma
38
(using the indexing sets
I=\{*\}\cup\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{F}
and
J=\emptyset
),
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{B}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
j\geq 1
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y\in\mathcal{G}
, and

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
. In particular, all these faces are compact.

Choose framed functions
(f_{*y},s_{*y})
on each
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
, and let
\mathcal{T}_{*y}=f_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
. Now define
r
by

r:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{G}\\
u-|y|=v+1\end{subarray}}\left[\mathcal{T}_{*y}\right]\cdot y\in CM_{u-v-1}\left(\mathcal{G};\Omega_{v+1}\right)

We next show that
(a,r)
is closed with respect to the mapping cone differential: explicitly, that
da=0
and
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})(a)+dr=0
. We have already seen that
da=0
.

We next define several auxiliary manifolds. For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|\leq v+1
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*y}
to be the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x^{\prime}x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)}

By Lemma
38
(with
I=\mathcal{F}
and
J=\mathcal{G}\setminus\{y\}
),
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*y}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{A}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
, and

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
.

For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+2
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{*y}
to be the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)}

By Lemma
37
, this is a manifold with corners.

For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+2
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}
to be the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}\\
|x|-|y|>0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x^{\prime}x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{F}\\
|x|-|y|>0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)}

By Lemma
38
(applied with
I=\left\{x\in\mathcal{F}\,|\,|x|-|y|>0\right\}
and
J=\left\{x\in\mathcal{F}\,|\,|x|-|y|=0\right\}\cup\left(\mathcal{G}\setminus\{y\}\right)
),
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{A}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

when for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
, when either
i+j>0
or
i=j=0
and
|x_{0}|-|y_{0}|>0
, and

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}

for
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
|x|-|y|=0
, and

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
when
j\geq 1
.

For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+2
, we define
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
to be the coequaliser of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\\
|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}_{*;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)\times[0,-\varepsilon)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{*y}\times[0,-\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\\
|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\\
|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{F};y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}\\
|y^{\prime}|-|y|>1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{x;y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{F}\\
|x|-|y|>0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}\\
|x|-|y|>0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}_{*;x^{\prime}x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

This is a manifold with corners by Lemma
37
; we see (either by iteratively applying Lemma
38
or by direct inspection of the corner strata) that
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
is a manifold with faces, with a system of faces given by

\mathcal{B}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
when either
j>1
or
j=0
and
|y_{0}|-|y_{1}|>1
, and

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*;x_{0}\ldots x_{i}}\times\mathcal{W}_{x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}\times\{0\}

for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
, when either
i+j>0
or
i=j=0
and
|x_{0}|-|y_{0}|>0
, and

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
with
j\geq 1
, and

\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*;y^{\prime}y}

for
y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
with
|y^{\prime}|-|y|=1
.

Both
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
contain faces of the form
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}
(for
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}
with
j\geq 1
), and in both cases these faces are non-compact but their intersections with the other boundary faces not of this form are always compact. By the collar neighbourhood theorem for manifolds with faces, there are embeddings
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{*y}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
onto neighbourhoods of all the faces of the form
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*;y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}
.

Pick a framed function
(h_{*y},w_{*y})
on
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{*y}
, and remove
h_{*y}^{-1}(-\infty,1)
from
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
to obtain
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
respectively. These are still manifolds with faces, but now they both have a boundary face identified with
h_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
, which is a compact smooth manifold with faces.

Now glue
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
along this common boundary face to obtain a new manifold with faces
\mathcal{V}_{*y}
. Its non-compact boundary faces are given by
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}
for
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
|x|-|y|=1
(coming from
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}^{r}
), and also other ones which are diffeomorphic to
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*;y^{\prime}y}
away from a neighbourhood of the compact faces of this face, for
y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
with
|y^{\prime}|-|y|=1
(coming from
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
).

For each
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+2
, pick framed functions
(g_{*y},t_{*y})
on
\mathcal{V}_{*y}
, agreeing with the framed functions
(f_{*x},s_{*x})
and
(f_{*y},s_{*y})
on appropriate faces.

Then
g_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
is a nullbordism of the manifold representing the
y
-coefficient of
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})(a)+dr
, so this chain must vanish, completing the proof that
(a,r)
is closed.

We proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem
109
. Since
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})
is a quasiisomorphism (over
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, which implies it is also one over
\Omega_{v+1}
), its cone is acyclic, so there is some
(a^{\prime},r^{\prime})
in

CM_{u-v-1}\left(\mathcal{F};\Omega_{v+1}\right)\oplus CM_{u-v}\left(\mathcal{G};\Omega_{v+1}\right)

such that
da^{\prime}=a
and
CM_{*}(\mathcal{W})(a^{\prime})+dr^{\prime}=r
.

Replacing each
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}
(for
u-|x|=v+1
) with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\sqcup a^{\prime}_{x}
(where
a^{\prime}_{x}
is a choice of some representative of the
x
-coefficient of
a
) (so the new
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is another extension of
\mathcal{A}
) and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
(for
u-|y|=v
) with
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}\sqcup r^{\prime}_{y}
, we may assume that the chain
(a,r)
is zero, i.e. that each
\mathcal{S}_{*x}
and each
\mathcal{T}_{*y}
is nullbordant.

We now define extensions of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
: explicitly, we define compact manifolds with faces
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}
for
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
u-|x|=v+2
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
for
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+1
, with systems of faces as described earlier.

For
x
in
\mathcal{F}
with
u-|x|=v+2
, let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{*x}=f_{*x}^{-1}(-\infty,1]
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{*x}=f_{*x}^{-1}\{1\}
is closed and nullbordant, we glue such a nullbordism onto this boundary face to obtain
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}
.

For
y
in
\mathcal{G}
with
u-|y|=v+1
, we let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}_{*y}=f_{*y}^{-1}(-\infty,1]
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{*y}=f_{*y}^{-1}\{1\}
is closed and nullbordant, we glue such a nullbordism onto this boundary face to obtain
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
, completing the inductive step.

Proof 5.15

(Incorporating framings)
.

Similarly to the proof of Theorem
111
, we coherently frame all manifolds arising in the unframed case to prove the framed case. We assume
\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are all framed, and we recall that each
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{*y}
is an
outgoing
boundary face of each
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y}
. We first equip each
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{*x}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{*y}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{*y}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{*y}
with compatible systems of framings in the same way as we did with
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}(\ldots)
in the proof of Lemma
70
; these then induce compatible systems of framings on each
\mathcal{S}_{*x}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
in the usual way.

We next frame each
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
. The only unbroken boundary face of
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
is
\mathcal{B}_{*y}\times\{0\}
; we declare this to be outgoing.

We first choose, inductively in
i-|y|
, stabilising bundles
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}
over each
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
, along with embeddings of the stabilising bundles of the stable framing of each boundary face of each
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
into
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}
, which agree on their overlaps.

We now define
st_{*y}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}:V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus V_{y}
as follows. Over each
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{*y}\times[0,-\varepsilon)
, we define
st^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
to be the composition

V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}\xrightarrow{\psi^{-1}}V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{*y}\oplus\mathbb{R}\sigma\xrightarrow{st^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\sigma\oplus V_{y}

extended to have stabilising bundle
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}
, where the first copy of
\mathbb{R}
comes from
st^{\mathcal{B}}
. On
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)
, we define
st^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
to be the composition

V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}\xrightarrow{\psi^{-1}}V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}_{*y^{\prime}}\oplus I^{\mathcal{G}}_{y^{\prime}y}\xrightarrow{st^{\mathcal{G}}\circ st^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\oplus V_{y}

and on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\times[0,\varepsilon)
, we define
st^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
to be the composition

V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}\xrightarrow{\psi^{-1}}V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}_{*x}\oplus I^{\mathcal{W}}_{xy}\xrightarrow{st^{\mathcal{W}}\circ st^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{y}

again extended to have stabilising bundle
\mathbb{T}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}
, where the first copy of
\mathbb{R}
in the final term comes from
st^{\mathcal{W}}
and the second from
st^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
.

We need these to agree on the regions where they overlap. Over all regions except those of the form
\mathcal{B}_{*y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)_{u}\times[0,-\varepsilon)_{v}
, this is identical to checking the gluing on overlaps in Lemma
70
; on the region
\mathcal{B}_{*y^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\times[0,\varepsilon)_{u}\times[0,-\varepsilon)_{v}
, it follows from a similar argument, using the following diagram.

\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus T\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{*y}\oplus\mathbb{R}\partial_{s}\oplus\mathbb{R}\tau_{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus\mathbb{R}\partial_{u}\oplus\mathbb{R}\partial_{v}\oplus T\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{*y^{\prime}}\oplus T\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\oplus\mathbb{R}\tau_{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\alpha}
\scriptstyle{d\beta}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus T\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{*y^{\prime}}\oplus\partial_{s}\oplus T\mathcal{G}_{y^{\prime}y}\oplus\mathbb{R}\tau_{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\psi}}
\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{*y}\oplus\mathbb{R}\sigma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}_{*y}}
\textstyle{V_{*[u]}\oplus I^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}_{*y^{\prime}}\oplus I^{\mathcal{G}}_{y^{\prime}y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}

Here the
\bar{\psi}
on the left is defined to send
\partial_{s}
to
\sigma
and is the identity on all other factors, and the
\bar{\psi}
on the right is defined to send
\partial_{s}
to
\tau_{y^{\prime}}
and is the identity on all other factors. Then we can see directly that this diagram commutes since all three ways of going from the top middle entry to the bottom middle entry send
\tau_{y}
to
\tau_{y}
,
\partial_{u}
to
\nu_{y^{\prime}}
and
\partial_{v}
to
-\nu^{\mathcal{B}}
(and are the identity on all other factors).

The
st^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
(along with the other framings) assemble to form systems of framings for all
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{*y}
, and similarly we may frame all
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{*y}
in exactly the same way. These framings then induce framings on each
\mathcal{T}_{*y}
and each
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
in the usual way.

The framings on
\mathcal{S}_{*x}
and
\mathcal{T}_{*y}
gives us a well-defined element

(a^{fr},r^{fr})\in CM_{u-v-2}\left(\mathcal{F};\Omega_{v+1}^{fr}\right)\oplus CM_{u-v-1}\left(\mathcal{G};\Omega^{fr}_{v+1}\right)

We have already seen that
da^{fr}=0
. We would like to show that the pair
(a^{fr},r^{fr})
is closed; in this case, an identical argument to before allows us to complete the induction step.

It therefore remains to show that
\mathcal{W}_{*}(a^{fr})-d(r^{fr})=0
. To do this we next equip
\mathcal{V}_{*y}
with appropriate framings. Note that both
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
have
h^{-1}_{*y}\{1\}
as an incoming face, so we can frame
\mathcal{V}_{*y}
by equipping it with the framing of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
over
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{r}_{*y}
and with the opposite framing of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
over
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{r}_{*y}
. Then
g^{-1}
is a framed nullbordism of

\bigsqcup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}_{*x}\times\mathcal{W}_{xy}\bigsqcup_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}}\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{*;y^{\prime}y}

from which the required closedness follows.

The analogous algebraic result is the following corollary of Hurewicz’ and Whitehead’s theorems:

Proposition 112
.

Let
R
be a
(-1)
-connected dga or ring spectrum, and
f:M\to N
a morphism in
R\modules
. If the induced map

f:M\otimes_{R}HR_{0}\to N\otimes_{R}HR_{0}

is an equivalence of
HR_{0}
-modules, then
f
is an equivalence of
R
-modules.

6
Baas-Sullivan singularities

For each
k>0
, we fix a set
\mathbb{M}^{k}
of closed
k
-manifolds
P
, each equipped with a stable framing
st^{P}:TP\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
with stabilising bundle
\mathbb{T}^{P}
, containing a representative of each isomorphism (i.e. diffeomorphism respecting stable framings) class of stably framed
k
-manifolds.

6.1
Singularities

Definition 113
.

A
manifold with faces with
k
-Baas-Sullivan singularities
(abbreviated to
with
\kBS
-singularities
, or just
\kBS
-manifold (with faces)
)
(M,N_{P},\iota)
(often just abbreviated to
M
) consists of a manifold with faces
M
, along with manifolds with faces
N_{P}
for each
P\in\mathbb{M}^{k}
, such that only finitely many
N_{P}
are non-empty, and
\iota
is a proper embedding of a boundary face

\iota:\bigsqcup\limits_{P\in\mathbb{M}^{k}}N_{P}\times P\hookrightarrow M

We call faces of
M
which have no components lying in the image of
\iota

essential faces
and faces of
M
which lie in the image of
\iota

singular faces
. We write
D_{M}
for the set of essential faces of
M
, and if
F\subseteq M
is an essential face, we write
D_{F\subseteq M}
for the set of essential faces containing
F
. Note that any essential face
F
of
M
naturally inherits the structure of a
\kBS
-manifold with faces. In particular, if
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime}_{P},\iota^{\prime})
is another
\kBS
-manifold with faces, for
M^{\prime}\hookrightarrow M
to be an inclusion of a face, there are inclusions of faces of non-singular manifolds
N_{P}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow N_{P}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{P}N_{P}^{\prime}\times P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\bigsqcup\limits_{P}N_{P}\times P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Note that in particular, even if
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}^{k}
are diffeomorphic, we do not treat them as the same singularity type.

A
closed
\kBS
-manifold
is a
\kBS
-manifold with faces which has no essential faces.

A
non-singular

\kBS
-manifold is one where all
N_{P}
are empty; this is just an ordinary manifold.

A
system of essential faces
for
M
is a collection of faces
F_{i}^{j}
is a collection of essential faces
F_{i}^{j}
of
M
such that each
F_{i}^{j}
has codimension
j
, all
F_{i}^{j}
have disjoint smooth loci, and any (positive codimension) essential face of
M
is a disjoint union of components of the
F_{i}^{j}
.

Let
X
be some other space. We say a continuous function
f:M\to X
is
compatible with the singularities
if, on each
\iota(N_{P}\times P)
,
f
is constant in the
P
direction, i.e. it only depends on the
N_{P}
-co-ordinate.

{rmk}

One should think of such a manifold as a singular manifold with singular loci locally modelled on cones over
k
-manifolds. Indeed taking
(M,N_{P},\iota)
a
\kBS
-manifold with faces and attaching cells
N_{P}\times C_{P}
to each
N_{P}\times P
(where
C_{P}
is the cone over
P
) gives such a singular manifold. Definition
113
can be thought of as removing neighbourhoods of the singular loci but keeping track of how to glue them back in; this definition is easier to work with in practice.

In particular, we think of the singular faces as part of a “singular locus” rather than actual faces of the manifold, whose role the essential faces play.

Lemma 114
.

Let
M
be a
\kBS
-manifold with faces,
G
a face of
M
, and
F
a face of
G
. Then if
G
is a singular face,
F
is too.

It follows from this that if
F
is an essential face,
G
is too.

Proof 6.1
.

If
G
lies in the image of
\iota
and
F\subset G
then
F
lies in the image of
\iota
.

{rmk}

A
\kBS
-manifold with faces of dimension
\leq k
is the same as a manifold with faces.

Definition 115
.

A framed function
(f,s)
on a
\kBS
-manifold
(M,N_{P},\iota)
is a framed function on the manifold with corners
M
such that over each
N_{P}\times P
,
f
is constant in the
P
-direction (so
f
is compatible with the singularities as a continuous function), and
s
is also constant in the
P
-direction (noting that since
f
is constant in the
P
-direction,
f^{-1}\{1\}\cap(N_{P}\times P)
naturally splits into a product of something with
P
).

The same proofs show that Lemma
43
(respectively Lemma
44
) holds for framed functions on
\kBS
-manifolds, where we restrict to (respectively extend from) only essential faces.

From the preimage theorem for manifolds with faces, we see that

Proposition 116
.

Let
(M,N_{P},\iota)
be a
\kBS
-manifold with face with a system of essential faces
\{F_{i}\}
, and
(f,s)
a framed function on
M
. Then
f^{-1}\{1\}
and
f^{-1}(-\infty,1]
are naturally
\kBS
-manifolds with faces.

There is a system of essential faces for
f^{-1}\{1\}
given by the
F_{i}\cap f^{-1}\{1\}
, and one for the
f^{-1}(-\infty,1]
given by the
F_{i}\cap f^{-1}\{1\}
and the
F_{i}\cap f^{-1}(-\infty,1]
.

Lemma 117
.

Let
M
be a
\kBS
-manifold with faces, and
N
a manifold with faces. Then
M\times N
and
N\times M
each canonically admit the structure of a manifold with faces with
\kBS
singularities.

Proof 6.2
.

M\times N
is a manifold with faces, and admits a canonical proper embedding of a codimension 1 face

\iota\times\id_{N}:\bigsqcup\limits_{P}(N_{P}\times N)\times P\hookrightarrow M\times N

where the
N_{P}
,
\iota
are as in Definition
113
. The case of
N\times M
is the same.

Corollary 118
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\kBS
-manifolds with faces, of dimensions
m
and
n
respectively. If either
m\leq k
or
n\leq k
, in particular if
2k>m+n
, then
M\times N
is naturally a
\kBS
-manifold with faces.

Proof 6.3
.

By Remark
6.1
one of
M
and
N
is non-singular, and so by Lemma
117
, their product is a
\kBS
-manifold with faces.

We have the following abstract gluing lemma, similarly to Lemma
37
(but now with essential faces playing the role of faces).

Let
(M_{i},Q_{i,P},\iota_{i})
be
\kBS
-manifolds (so each
\iota_{i}:\sqcup_{P}Q_{i,P}\times P\hookrightarrow M_{i}
is the inclusion of a boundary face). Let
\alpha_{w}:N_{w}\hookrightarrow M_{i_{w}}
and
\beta_{w}:N_{w}\hookrightarrow M_{j_{w}}
be inclusions of essential boundary faces; each of these induces the structure of a manifold with
\kBS
-singularities on
N_{w}
, and we assume these structures agree. We write
(N_{w},R_{w,P},\iota_{w})
for this structure.

In particular, we have diffeomorphisms
R_{w,P}\simeq\partial Q_{i_{w},P}\simeq\partial Q_{j_{w},P}
.

We now have inclusions of boundary faces
\alpha_{w}:R_{w,P}\hookrightarrow Q_{i,P}
and
\beta_{w}:R_{w,P}\hookrightarrow Q_{j,P}
as in the hypothesis of Lemma
37
; for each
P
we let
Y_{P}
be the coequaliser of the diagram

\bigsqcup\limits_{w}R_{w,P}\times[0,\varepsilon)^{2}\rightrightarrows\bigsqcup\limits_{i}Q_{i,P}\times[0,\varepsilon)

which is a manifold with corners by Lemma
37
.

Let
Z
be constructed as in Lemma
37
. There are natural disjoint embeddings
\iota_{j}:Y_{P}\times P\hookrightarrow Z
given by gluing together the
\iota_{i}
.

Lemma 119
.

(Z,Y_{P},\iota:=\sqcup_{P}\iota_{P})
is a manifold with corners with
\kBS
-singularities.

Proof 6.4
.

Lemma
37
shows that
Z
is a manifold with corners. Equipping this with the map
\iota
defines a system of singular faces which makes this a manifold with corners with
\kBS
-singularities.

Furthermore if we can index the
M_{i}
and
N_{w}
as in Lemma
38
, the statement of Lemma
38
still holds for a system of essential faces, with the same proof.

Definition 120
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be flow categories. A
\kBS
-pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism

\mathcal{W}
from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
is the same as a
pre
-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphism except all manifolds are allowed to have
\kBS
-singularities, and all systems of faces are essential systems of faces.

More explicitly, this consists of compact
\kBS
-manifolds with faces, say
\mathcal{W}_{xy}=(\mathcal{W}_{xy},N_{xy,P},\iota_{xy})
(of dimension
|x|-|y|
) for all
x
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
|x|-|y|\leq n
, along with maps

c=c^{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{F}_{xz}\times\mathcal{W}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{xy}

and

c=c^{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}_{xz}\times\mathcal{G}_{zy}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{xy}

which are compatible with
c^{\mathcal{F}}
and
c^{\mathcal{G}}
, which define a system of essential faces

\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}

for
x_{0}=x,\ldots,x_{i}
in
\mathcal{F}
and
y_{0},\ldots,y_{j}=y
in
\mathcal{G}
.

We define
\kBS
-
\tau_{\leq n}
-pre-bordisms
and
\kBS
-
\tau_{\leq n}
-bordisms
analogously.

There are similar definitions of flow categories with
\kBS
-singularities, bilinear maps, associators etc., but we will not use them.

{rmk}

Note that since the flow categories
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
don’t have any singularities, the products
\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}\ldots x_{i};y_{0}\ldots y_{j}}
admit the structure of manifolds with
\kBS
singularities, by Lemma
118
. In practice we will only use these definitions when
2k-1>n
, so that we can compose these: if we did not have this inequality, then when one attempts to compose such things, eventually one will have to take a product of two manifolds with non-empty singular faces, which does not have
\kBS
singularities in the sense of the definition we’ve given. Compare to Remark
1.3
.

Composition of pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms with
\kBS
-singularities can be defined the same as in the non-singular case, with exactly the same proofs of associativity and well-definedness, except all framed functions which are chosen must be chosen to be compatible with the singularities. Composing pre-
\tau_{\leq n}
-morphisms with
\kBS
-singularities with bilinear maps is entirely analogous.

{rmk}

In order to avoid the above truncation issue, we could amend our definition to allow boundary faces of the form
N\times P_{1}\times\ldots\times P_{k}
where each
P_{i}\in\mathbb{M}^{k}
, in which case products of manifolds with this structure still have this structure; this however is not necessary for the current application.

One can make an analogous construction for Baas-Sullivan singularities modelled after any set of closed manifolds
\mathbb{M}
; we conjecture that the resulting category is equivalent to (the homotopy category of) the category of perfect modules over
R
, where
R
is the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-quotient of
MO
by each
[M]\in\Omega_{*}\simeq\pi_{*}MO
for
M\in\mathbb{M}
.

6.2
Bordism with singularities

Let
X
be a space.

Definition 121
.

Let
M
and
N
be two closed manifolds with
\kBS
-singularities. A
\kBS
-bordism
from
M
to
N
is a compact manifold
W
with faces with
\kBS
-singularities, with a system of essential faces given by
\{M,N\}
.

The
i
th
\kBS
-bordism group of
X
,
\Omega^{\kBS}_{i}(X)
, is the set of pairs
(M,f)
, where
M
is a closed manifold with
\kBS
-singularities and
f:M\rightarrow X
is a continuous map which is compatible with the singularities, modulo the equivalence relation
\sim
, where we say
(M,f)\sim(M^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if there is a
\kBS
-bordism
W
from
M
to
M^{\prime}
, along with a map
F:W\rightarrow X
extending
f
and
f^{\prime}
which is again compatible with the singularities.

We write
\Omega_{i}^{\kBS}
and
\Omega_{i}
for
\Omega_{i}^{\kBS}(*)
and
\Omega_{i}(*)
respectively. Recall that
\Omega_{*}(X)
is naturally an
\Omega_{*}
-module, though
\Omega^{\kBS}_{*}
is not necessarily a ring.

Proposition 122
.

Let
X
be any space. The group
\Omega_{k}^{\kBS}(X)
is zero.

Proof 6.5
.

Let
f:M\to X
represent a class in
\Omega_{k}^{\kBS}(X)
.
M
is a closed
k
-dimensional
\kBS
-manifold, and so is non-singular.

Let
W=M\times[0,1]
, viewed as a
\kBS
-manifold with singular boundary face
M\times\{1\}
, and
g:W\to X
given by
f
on the first factor. This is a bounding
\kBS
-manifold witnessing the vanishing of
[(f,M)]
.

Proposition 123
.

Let
X
be any space. The natural map
\theta:\Omega_{k+1}(X)\rightarrow\Omega_{k+1}^{\kBS}(X)
which sends
(M,f:M\to X)
to itself, has kernel
\Omega_{k}\cdot\Omega_{1}(X)
.

Proof 6.6
.

Choose a class in
\ker\theta
, represented by a pair
(M,f:M\to X)
, where
M
is a closed
k
-manifold and
f
is a continuous map. Then there is some
\kBS
-manifold
(W,N_{P},\iota)
with faces, such that its only essential face is
M
(and itself), and a map
F:W\to X
which is compatible with the singularities. This means that, viewed as a smooth manifold (i.e. without
\kBS
-singularities),
(W,F)
is a bordism from
(M,f)
to

\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{P}N_{P}\times P,F\right)

and so we must have that

[(M,f)]=\sum\limits_{P}[(N_{P},F)]\cdot[P]

in
\Omega_{k+1}(X)
, using the fact that on each
N_{P}\times P
,
F
is constant in the
P
direction. Clearly the right hand side lives in
\Omega_{k}\cdot\Omega_{1}(X)
.

Conversely, any product
N\times Q
of a 1-manifold
N
and some
Q\in\mathbb{M}^{k}
